Restricted Gauge Theory Formalism and Unimodular Gravity
A. O. Barvinsky, D. V. Nesterov

Abstract
(Received 29 December 2022; revised 25 May 2023; accepted 25 May 2023; published 7 September 2023) We develop a Lagrangian quantization formalism for a class of theories obtained by the restriction of the configuration space of gauge fields from a wider (parental) gauge theory. This formalism is based on application of the Batalin-Vilkovisky technique for quantization of theories with linearly dependent generators, their linear dependence originating from a special type of projection from the originally irreducible gauge generators of the parental theory. The algebra of these projected generators is shown to be closed for parental gauge algebras closed off shell. We demonstrate that new physics of the restricted theory, as compared to its parental theory, is associated with the rank deficiency of a special gauge-restriction operator reflecting the gauge transformations of the restriction constraints functions — this distinguishes the restricted theory from its partial gauge fixing. As a byproduct of this technique a workable algorithm for the one-loop effective action in generic first-stage reducible theory was constructed, along with the explicit set of tree-level Ward identities for gauge field, ghost, and ghosts-for-ghosts propagators. The formalism is applied to unimodular gravity theory, and its one-loop effective action is obtained in terms of functional determinants of minimal second-order operators, calculable on generic backgrounds by Schwinger-DeWitt technique of local curvature expansion. The result is shown to be equivalent to Einstein gravity theory with a cosmological term up to a special contribution of the global degree of freedom associated with the variable value of the cosmological constant. The role of this degree of freedom in a special duality relation between Einstein theory and unimodular gravity is briefly discussed. DOI: 10.1103/PhysRevD.108.065004

I
Introduction

The purpose of this paper is to discuss an interesting property that arises when one restricts a configuration space of gauge theory. This is the origin of a reducible gauge structure characterized by linear dependence of the generators of gauge transformations. Both mechanisms — reduction of the configuration space and reducibility of gauge symmetry — are widely known and applied in various areas of field theory. In the gravity theory context one of the most important examples of the field space restriction is unimodular gravity (UMG) theory which was suggested by Einstein soon after the invention of general relativity
Einstein_UMG
. Much later it was considered in the context of particle physics
Bij-vanDam-Ng
, in the context of a spacetime covariant formulation
Henneaux_T
, as a problem of time and the cosmological constant problem
Unruh
, and then applied within the dark energy paradigm
Shaposhnikov
with the emphasis on purely technical issues of perturbation theory, etc. Extension beyond the unimodular constraint on the metric of spacetime — the so-called generalized unimodular gravity
Barvinsky:2017pmm
— also turned out to be rather fruitful from the viewpoint of the generation of viable dark energy and inflation scenarios
Barvinsky:2019agh
;
Barvinsky:2019qzx
.

From a field-theoretical point of view UMG is interesting because it is generally observationally equivalent to general relativity (see recent review
Carballo-Rubio:2022ofy
), but even semiclassically raises the issues of such an equivalence
Alvarez:2005iy
depending on the subtleties of local physics vs global behavior encoded in boundary conditions, finiteness of the spacetime volume, thermodynamical setup in gravity theory
Fiol:2008vk
, etc. Unimodular gravity is an interesting object of group theoretical analysis
Alvarez:2006uu
and quantization
deLeonArdon:2017qzg
;
Percacci:2017fkn
;
Percacci:2017fsy
;
Kugo:2022iob
;
Kugo:2022dui
, especially regarding its relation to quantization of Einstein theory
Bufalo:2015wda
as a sort of a parental theory whose field space reduction leads to the unimodular gravity model.

On the other hand, it has been conjectured
Barvinsky:2017pmm
that the formalism of quantized unimodular and generalized unimodular gravity theories can be developed along the lines of gauge theory quantization in models with linearly dependent (or reducible) generators
Batalin:1983ggl
;
Batalin:1983pz
. This observation follows from a simple fact that the restriction of the configuration space of metrics to the subspace of metrics with a unit determinant results in the relevant reduction in the space of diffeomorphism invariance parameters, which can be attained by a projection procedure. Then, if one wants (for the sake of retaining manifest covariance) to describe the restricted theory in terms of the parental one, this projection applied to the original gauge generators makes them linearly dependent.

To the best of our knowledge this relation between the reducible nature of gauge generators and the reduction of the field space has not yet been fully exploited even in the unimodular gravity context, as well as its extension to more or less general gauge theories. The studies of theories with restrictions on gauge transformations, the theories with the so-called unfree gauge parameters, were recently intensively conducted in a series of publications

Kaparulin:2019quz
;
Lyakhovich:unfree-hamiltonian

with the purpose of constructing the BFV (Batalin-Fradkin-Vilkovisky) formalism transcending these restrictions to the ghost sector of the model and starting from the first principles of Hamiltonian formalism and canonical quantization. Here we choose a somewhat different approach (closer to the Lagrangian formalism) and try to develop the idea of
Barvinsky:2017pmm
for a generic parental gauge field theory with the closed algebra of its irreducible generators becoming linearly dependent in the course of a projection induced by the restriction of the original configuration space.

Our method is based on the explicit construction of a projector operator with which the generators of the original parental theory are projected onto the generators of the residual symmetries of the restricted theory. Linear dependence of the latter is then treated by the quantization method of Batalin and Vilkovisky (BV) for reducible gauge theories
Batalin:1983ggl
;
Batalin:1983pz
— a brief review of this formalism with all necessary notations and definitions is presented in Sec.
II.1
. As a by-product of using BV formalism we develop in much detail the gauge-fixing procedure for gauge theory of the first-stage reducibility. Quite interestingly, despite numerous applications of this method, current literature does not present a workable algorithm even for the one-loop approximation in generic reducible gauge theories. Implicitly this algorithm is, of course, contained in the pioneering works
Batalin:1983ggl
;
Batalin:1983pz
, but its concrete realization with all the details of Ward identities providing gauge independence of the on-shell effective action is still missing. We close this omission and suggest the recipe for all the elements of Feynman diagrammatic technique and the relevant algorithm of the one-loop effective action in the above class of theories at the level of presentation characteristic of a folklore use of the well-known Faddeev-Popov prescription.

The gauge-fixing algorithm here turns out to be more involved than in a conventional Faddeev-Popov formalism for irreducible theories, because it includes gauge fixing for original gauge fields, gauge fixing for ghosts and the corresponding ghosts for ghosts. Even for first-stage reducible theories (when there is no higher-order zero vectors for reducibility generators — zero vectors of original gauge generators) this gauge-fixing procedure involves, apart from the usual gauge conditions, at least three extra gauge-fixing elements and the construction of a special projector in the gauge-breaking term of the theory. The main result for the one-loop effective action of gauge theories of the first-stage reducibility is assembled in the sequence of Eqs. (
2.49
)–(
2.53
) of Sec.
II.3
.

Then in Sec.
III
we explain how a reducible gauge structure with linearly-dependent generators arises in theories with restricted configuration space of gauge fields. In particular, we raise the question why and when constraining this space can be regarded merely as a partial gauge fixing, or on the contrary, forming a new physical theory inequivalent to the parental one. It turns out that the answer is based on the rank of a special
gauge-restriction operator
. The rank deficiency of this operator, or the presence of its nontrivial zero modes, signifies new physics of a restricted theory as compared to the parental model whose field space is subject to restriction. This gauge-restriction operator allows one to construct the projector on the space of residual gauge symmetries. The projected gauge generators form the algebra which is closed as long as the original parental theory has a closed algebra both on and off shell.

The zero vectors of this projector become first-stage reducibility generators of the BV method of treatment of the restricted theory, and they represent a free element of the formalism, whose choice is limited only by a certain rank restriction condition. It turns out, however, that their special normalization fully provides on-shell independence of the one-loop effective action of this choice, whereas the normalization of the gauge generators of the parental theory can be fixed by matching it with the canonical formalism and canonical quantization of parental theory.

The one-loop effective action for generic restricted theory is discussed in Sec.
IV.1
. Discussion of correct normalization of the gauge generators in a covariant formalism is the subject of Secs.
IV.2
and

IV.3
. In Sec.
IV.4
we discuss the factor in one-loop effective action which distinguishes restricted and parental theories at the quantum level.

In Sec.
V
we apply the above formalism to the unimodular gravity theory treated as descending from its parental version — Einstein general relativity. We construct all the elements of its gauge-fixing procedure in the background covariant gauge
DeWitt
with the background covariance property extended to all the objects of the Feynman diagrammatic technique. Then, according to the general algorithm, we build on shell the one-loop effective action of the UMG theory. This confirms conclusions of
Percacci:2017fsy
which were attained, however, with certain assumptions on the treatment of the group of diffeomorphisms volume factored out of the partition function. No such assumptions are needed in our approach which is fully determined by the requirement of gauge independence of the on-shell physical results.

Moreover, our approach allows us to disentangle the one-loop contribution of a global zero mode responsible for the “new” physics contained in the UMG theory as compared to general relativity. The UMG one-loop effective action coincides with that of the Einstein theory having a cosmological constant modulo a special contribution of the global degree of freedom associated with the variable value of this constant. The resulting expression for the effective action, which is usually presented in terms of functional determinants on irreducible (transverse and transverse-traceless) subspaces of the full tensor and vector field space, is built in terms of the functional determinants of the minimal operators. These determinants admit for their calculation the application of the Schwinger-DeWitt curvature expansion
Schwinger-DeWitt
. This property is important for calculations on generic backgrounds not exhausted by homogeneous spaces.

In the Conclusion we summarize the results and briefly discuss the role of a global extra degree of freedom in the UMG theory as a cosmological constant. In particular, we show how this reveals the duality of UMG and Einstein theories analogous to the transition between statistical ensembles with fixed observables related by the Laplace duality transformations. In the appendixes we discuss the Moore-Penrose construction of inverse operators for degenerate operators
MoorePenrose
, as well as the derivation of the gauge algebra of the projected generators and some technical details of determinant relations needed for the construction of the generator basis.

II
Effective action for theories with first-stage reducible gauge generators

In this section we briefly review Batalin-Vilkovisky (BV) formalism for the first-stage reducible gauge theories. Throughout this section (and in the most of other sections unless otherwise indicated) we use DeWitt condensed notations. All indices are of condensed nature and combine discrete bundle and continuous spacetime-point labels. Two-index quantities are two-point kernels and summation over contracted indices implies the corresponding spacetime integration. Ranges of condensed indices reflect the continuum of spacetime points and the discrete spin-tensor labels of fields. The ranks of two-point kernels of linear operators and forms thus refer to their functional space dimensionality.

II.1
Batalin-Vilkovisky formalism for reducible gauge theories

Consider a generic gauge theory of fields
\phi^{i}
(with the range of field indices
{i}
formally denoted in what follows by
n
). Let it be described by the action
S[\,\phi\,]
which is invariant under the local gauge transformations with generators
\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha}(\phi)
and infinitesimal gauge parameters
\xi^{\alpha}
(the range of gauge indices
\alpha
being denoted by
m_{0}<n
)

\displaystyle\delta_{\xi}\phi^{i}=\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha},

(2.1)

\displaystyle S_{,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha}=0,\;\quad\;S_{,{i}}\equiv\frac{\delta S}{\delta\phi^{i}}.

(2.2)

Here, and in what follows, comma denotes a functional derivative with respect to the relevant field variable.

The algebra of gauge generators begins with the relations — corollaries of the Jacobi identity for a double commutator of gauge transformations

\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha,{j}}\mathcal{R}^{j}_{\beta}-\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\beta,{j}}\mathcal{R}^{j}_{\alpha}\,=\,\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\gamma}C^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}+E^{{i}{j}}_{\alpha\beta}S_{,{j}}

(2.3)

with the structure functions
C^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}(\phi)=-C^{\gamma}_{\beta\alpha}(\phi)
,
E^{{i}{j}}_{\alpha\beta}(\phi)=-E^{{i}{j}}_{\beta\alpha}(\phi)=-E^{{j}{i}}_{\alpha\beta}(\phi)
. For closed algebras (in contrast to so-called open algebras)
E^{{i}{j}}_{\alpha\beta}(\phi)
and higher-order structure functions of the gauge algebra vanish
Henneaux:1992ig
;
Gomis:1994he
.

The theory is
reducible
when its gauge generators are linearly dependent on shell, that is on solutions of classical equations
S_{,{i}}=0
, so that there exist
m_{1}
reducibility generators
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}=\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}(\phi)
— right zero-eigenvalue eigenvectors of the original generators
\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha}
parametrized by indices
{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
of range
m_{1}
. This implies relations

\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha}\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,=\,-B^{{i}{j}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}S_{,{j}}

(2.4)

with some coefficient functions
B^{{i}{j}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}(\phi)=-B^{{j}{i}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}(\phi)
. For the
first-stage
reducible theories, which we consider in this section, the generators
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
form on shell a complete independent set, whereas for higher-order stages of reducibility they also become linearly dependent with higher-order reducibility generators and so on.

Master action

Batalin-Vilkovisky quantization of gauge theories starts with the construction of
master action

S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}[\,\varPhi,\varPhi^{*}]
which is a certain extension of the original action classical
S[\,\phi\,]
onto the configuration space of fields and antifields
(\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}},\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}})
. This even-dimensional space is
\mathbb{Z}
-graded with respect to the so-called ghost number
\mathop{gh}{(\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}})}=-\mathop{gh}{(\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}})}-1
and
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
-graded with respect to their Grassmann parity
\epsilon
,
\epsilon(\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}})=\epsilon(\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}})+1
. Original fields
\phi^{i}
become a part of extended fields set
\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}
and carry zero ghost number. To avoid messy sign factors we assume that all
\phi^{i}
are bosonic (even) fields,
\epsilon(\phi^{i})=0
, and Grassmann parity of ghost fields and antifields coincides with
\mathbb{Z}_{2}
parity of their ghost number (those with even ghost numbers are commuting fields, those with odd ghosts number — anticommuting).

Extension from
S[\,\phi\,]
to
S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}[\,\varPhi,\varPhi^{*}]
is governed by the
master equation
on
S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}[\,\varPhi,\varPhi^{*}]

{\mathchoice{\big(S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}{\hskip 0.4pt,\hskip 0.4pt}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}\big)}{\big(S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}{\hskip 0.4pt,\hskip 0.4pt}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}\big)}{(S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}\,,\,S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}})}{(S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}},S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}})}}\,\equiv\,\frac{{\delta^{(r)}}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta{\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}}}\frac{{\delta^{(l)}}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta{\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}}}-\frac{{\delta^{(r)}}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta{\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}}}\frac{{\delta^{(l)}}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta{\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}}}\,=\,0

(2.5)

in terms of the antibracket
(G,H)

Batalin:1981jr
which is defined for any two functionals
G
and
H
of fields and antifields. Here
(r)
and
(l)
label, respectively, right and left derivatives.

The solution
S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}[\,\varPhi,\varPhi^{*}]
is obtained as a perturbative expansion in powers of ghost fields and antifields. To make this solution proper and allow one to perform a further gauge-fixing procedure one should specify a BV-extended configuration space and impose certain initial conditions to (
2.5
) which should explicitly encode the gauge structure of the original action
S[\,\phi\,]
.

Any proper solution of master equation is inevitably gauge invariant

Batalin:1983ggl
;
Henneaux:1992ig
;
Batalin:1981jr
;
Henneaux:1989jq
;
Gomis:1994he
;
BV3
. In terms of the total set of BV fields and antifields
\varPhi\vphantom{\big|}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\!BV}}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}=(\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}},\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}})
,
{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}=1,..,2N
,
{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}=1,..,N
, the master equation reads

\frac{{\delta^{r}}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta\varPhi\vphantom{\big|}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\!BV}}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}}\zeta^{{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathchoice{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{B}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}}}\frac{{\delta^{l}}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta\varPhi\vphantom{\big|}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\!BV}}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{B}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}}}=0,\;\quad\;\zeta^{{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathchoice{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{B}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}}}=\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\;\;\;1\\
-1&\;\;\;0\\
\end{array}\right].

(2.6)

By differentiating (
2.6
) one gets the Noether identities

\frac{{\delta^{r}}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta\varPhi\vphantom{\big|}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\!BV}}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}}\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\,{\mathchoice{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{C}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathcal{C}}}}=0,\;\quad\;\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\,{\mathchoice{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{C}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathcal{C}}}}=\zeta^{{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathchoice{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{B}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}}}\frac{\delta^{l}\delta^{r}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta\varPhi\vphantom{\big|}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\!BV}}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{B}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}}\delta\varPhi\vphantom{\big|}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\!BV}}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{C}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathcal{C}}}},

(2.7)

featuring
2N
gauge generators
\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\,{\mathchoice{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{C}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathcal{C}}}}
of master action gauge symmetry.

These generators, however, form a reducible set because
the second variational derivative of (
2.6
)
shows their
N
-reducibility on shell,

\zeta^{-1}_{{\mathchoice{\mathcal{D}}{\mathcal{D}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{D}}}{\mathcal{D}}}{\mathchoice{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{B}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}}}\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{B}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}}_{\,{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}}\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}_{\,{\mathchoice{\mathcal{C}}{\mathcal{C}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{C}}}{\mathcal{C}}}}\propto\delta S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}/\delta\varPhi\vphantom{\big|}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\!BV}}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}=0
.
Thus, the master action
S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}

possess
N
on-shell independent symmetries — half the dimension of the BV configuration space
(\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}},\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}})
.

1
1

1

Nilpotence of
2N\times 2N
matrix implies that its rank is at most
N
. Considering a
proper
solution implies that rank of the Hessian
\delta^{l}\delta^{r}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}/\delta\varPhi\vphantom{\big|}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\!BV}}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{A}}{\mathcal{A}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{A}}}{\mathcal{A}}}\delta\varPhi\vphantom{\big|}_{\scriptscriptstyle{\!BV}}^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{B}}{\mathcal{B}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{B}}}{\mathcal{B}}}
equals
N
. Otherwise the set of Noether identities (
2.7
) is incomplete and there are more then
N
gauge generators since there are more then
N
zero modes of the Hessian.

Convenient gauge fixing of the master action gauge symmetry (
2.7
)
consists in expressing antifields in terms of fields via
N
gauge conditions

Batalin:1983ggl
;
Henneaux:1992ig
;
Batalin:1981jr
;
Henneaux:1989jq
;
Gomis:1994he
;
BV3

\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}-\frac{\delta\varPsi[\,\varPhi\,]}{\delta\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}}=0.

(2.8)

It is parametrized by a single functional of fields — gauge fermion
\varPsi[\,\varPhi\,]
of ghost number
-1
and leads to gauge-fixed action

S_{\varPsi}[\,\varPhi\,]=S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}\big[\,\varPhi\hskip 1.0pt,\delta\varPsi[\hskip 1.0pt\varPhi\hskip 1.0pt]/\delta\varPhi\,\big],

(2.9)

which is functional of only BV fields
\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}
. For the correctly chosen gauge fermion the gauge-fixed action (
2.9
) appears to be a nondegenerate

\mathrm{sdet}\,[\,\delta^{(l)}\delta^{(r)}S_{\varPsi}/\delta\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}\,\delta\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{J}}{\mathcal{J}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{J}}}{\mathcal{J}}}\,]_{\,\delta S_{\varPsi}/\delta\varPhi=0}\neq 0

and yields a perturbatively consistent path integral for the generating functional which modulo the contribution of local measure reads

Z=\int D\varPhi\;e^{\,i\,S_{\varPsi}[\,\varPhi\,]}.

(2.10)

The master equation for
S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}[\,\varPhi,\varPhi^{*}]
(
2.5
) provides the corner stone of gauge theory quantization — independence of the on-shell generating functional of the choice of gauge fermion. Under the gauge fermion change
\varPsi\to\varPsi+\Delta\varPsi
, in view of integration by parts the change in
Z
reads

\displaystyle\Delta Z

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\int D\varPhi\,\Delta\varPsi\,\left(\,i\,\frac{\delta^{(r)}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}}\frac{\delta^{(l)}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}}\right.

(2.11)

\displaystyle\quad\left.\,+\,\frac{\delta^{(r)}}{\delta\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}}\frac{\delta^{(l)}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}}{\delta\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}}\right)\Big|_{\,\varPhi^{*}=\frac{\delta\varPsi}{\delta\varPhi}}\,e^{\,i\,S_{\varPsi}},

where extra terms vanish in view of Grassmann symmetry properties. This expression equals zero because the first term vanishes in view of the master equation
\delta^{(r)}\!S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}\!\!/\delta\varPhi^{*}_{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}\>\delta^{(l)}\!S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}\!\!/\delta\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}=\tfrac{1}{2}(S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}},S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}})
, while the second term in local theories is proportional to
\partial...\partial\,\delta(0)
and is supposed to be either canceled by a local measure or killed by dimensional regularization. The local measure is not rigorously available within the BV formalism whose incompleteness can be disregarded for theories with local gauge algebra and within the class of local gauge fermions. This measure can be attained within the canonical BFV quantization formalism, which will be used below to justify the application of the BV method for
restricted
gauge theories in which locality of their generators will be spoiled by generically nonlocal projectors.

Minimal and nonminimal proper solutions

Construction of BV master action begins with finding a proper solution of master equation (
2.5
) on the
minimal
sector of fields and antifields. For the first-stage reducible theories (
2.2
),(
2.4
) this requires introduction of ghosts
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}
,
\mathop{gh}{(\mathcal{C}^{\alpha})}=1
, the ghosts for ghosts
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
,
\mathop{gh}{(\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}})}=2
, and antifields to all fields and ghosts
Batalin:1983ggl
;
Henneaux:1992ig
;
Gomis:1994he
,

\varPhi_{\rm min}=(\phi^{i},\mathcal{C}^{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}),\quad\varPhi^{*}_{\rm min}=(\phi^{*}_{i},\mathcal{C}^{*}_{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{*}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}})

(2.12)

The
minimal
proper solution
S^{\rm min}[\,\varPhi_{\rm min},\varPhi^{*}_{\rm min}\,]
of the master equation can be represented as the series in powers of antifields

S^{\rm min}\,=\,S+\phi^{*}_{i}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{C}^{*}_{\alpha}\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}+...

(2.13)

where
S=S[\,\phi\,]
is the action of the initial gauge theory, two terms bilinear in ghost fields and antifields serve as initial conditions for the master equation which guaranty that such a solution of master equation is proper,
\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha}
and
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
are gauge and reducibility generators (
2.2
),(
2.4
), dots stand for higher-order terms in powers of ghost fields and antifields. All higher-order terms can be found within the iterative procedure of solving the master equation which leads to
Henneaux:1992ig
;
Gomis:1994he

\displaystyle S^{min}

\displaystyle\,=\,

\displaystyle S\,+\phi^{*}_{i}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}+\mathcal{C}^{*}_{\alpha}\left(\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}+\tfrac{1}{2}C_{\beta\gamma}^{\alpha}\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}\right)

\displaystyle+\mathcal{C}^{*}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\left(A_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}\alpha}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}+\tfrac{1}{2}F_{\alpha\beta\gamma}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}\right)

\displaystyle+\phi^{*}_{i}\phi^{*}_{j}\left({\tfrac{1}{2}B_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}^{{j}{i}}\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}+\tfrac{1}{4}E_{\alpha\beta}^{{j}{i}}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}}\right)+\ldots\,,

where the coefficient functions of higher-order terms originate from higher-order structure functions of gauge algebra and the dots denote terms with antifields of the total ghost number
-3
and lower.

Meaningful gauge fixing of the BV master action requires extension of the proper solution to nonminimal configuration space. Motivation for this is that gauge fixing (
2.8
)
is performed through properly constructed
gauge fermion

\varPsi[\,\varPhi\,]
— the functional of only the BV fields
\varPhi^{\mathchoice{\mathcal{I}}{\mathcal{I}}{\scriptstyle{\mathcal{I}}}{\mathcal{I}}}
. But the gauge fermion has a negative ghost number
-1
and thus cannot be constructed as a functional of the minimal fields
\phi^{i},\mathcal{C}^{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
with nonnegative ghost numbers. Therefore, the set of fields should be further extended to contain auxiliary ghosts with negative ghost numbers.

Standard scheme for first-stage reducible theories presumes introducing auxiliary ghosts
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
, their partners
\pi_{\alpha},\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
with the higher ghost number and the corresponding set of antifields
Henneaux:1992ig
;
Gomis:1994he
, so that nonminimal BV configuration space reads

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\varPhi

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underbrace{\phi^{i},\mathcal{C}^{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\varPhi_{\rm min}},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}},\pi_{\alpha},\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}),

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\varPhi^{*}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(\underbrace{\phi^{*}_{i},\mathcal{C}^{*}_{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{*}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\varPhi^{*}_{\rm min}},{\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}^{\alpha},{\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime*}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}},{\pi^{*}}^{\alpha}\!,{\pi^{*}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\!,{\pi^{\prime*}}_{\!\!\!{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}).

(2.14)

Here the fields
\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
are often referred to as antighosts,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
— extraghosts,
\pi_{\alpha},\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
— Lagrange multiplier or Nakanishi-Lautrup fields.

, where antifields
{\pi^{*}}^{\alpha},{\pi^{*}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},{\pi^{\prime*}}_{\!\!\!{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
are omitted since they do not appear in the BV procedure.

BV configuration space of the first-stage reducible gauge theory.

The
nonminimal
proper solution
S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}[\,\varPhi,\varPhi^{*}]
of the master equation on such space is just a sum of the minimal master action
S^{\rm min}[\,\varPhi_{\rm min},\varPhi^{*}_{\rm min}]
(
2.13
) and the contribution of auxiliary fields
S^{\rm aux}[\pi_{\alpha},\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}},{\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}^{\alpha},{\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\mathcal{C}^{\prime*}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}]

\displaystyle S^{\scriptscriptstyle{BV}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S^{\rm min}+S^{\rm aux},

(2.15)

\displaystyle S^{\rm aux}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\pi_{\alpha}{\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}^{\alpha}+\,\pi_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}{\bar{\mathcal{C}}^{*}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}+\,\mathcal{C}^{\prime*}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}.

(2.16)

The master action (
2.15
) obviously satisfies the master equation (
2.5
) on the nonminimal configuration space since auxiliary and minimal sectors are so far decoupled (these sectors will be mixed after gauge fixing) so that
(S^{\rm min}+S^{\rm aux},S^{\rm min}+S^{\rm aux})=(S^{\rm min},S^{\rm min})+(S^{\rm aux},S^{\rm aux})
, and by construction
S^{\rm min}
and
S^{\rm aux}
separately nilpotent in antibracket.

Choice of a gauge fermion for the Gaussian gauge fixing

Grading restriction
\mathop{gh}{(\varPsi)}=-1
on gauge fermion
\varPsi[\,\varPhi\,]
admits the following form at most quadratic in ghosts and auxiliary fields

\displaystyle\varPsi[\,\varPhi\,]\;

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}\big(\chi^{\alpha}(\phi)+\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}(\phi)\,\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\big)+\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\,\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\alpha}(\phi)\,\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}

(2.17)

\displaystyle+\tfrac{1}{2}\,\big(\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}\pi_{\beta}+\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}{+\,}\pi_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\big).\;\;\;\;

The latter group of terms linear in
\pi_{\alpha},\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
allows us to implement
Gaussian
(Faddeev-Popov) type of gauge fixing when
\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}
,
\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
are
nondegenerate

\det{\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}}\neq 0,\qquad\det{\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}}\neq 0.

(2.18)

In generic theories it is convenient to choose
\phi
-independent coefficient functions
\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}
,
\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
. Such a choice guarantees the possibility to integrate out auxiliary fields
\pi_{\alpha},\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
to obtain Faddeev-Popov representation for the generating functional.

Here we take into account that for
\phi
-dependent functions
\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}(\phi),\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}(\phi)
terms linear in companion fields
\pi_{\alpha},\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
will arise in gauge-fixed minimal master action
S_{\varPsi}^{\rm min}
instead of each antifield,
\phi^{*}_{i}=\delta\varPsi/\delta\phi^{i}=\tfrac{1}{2}{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}(\delta\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}/\delta\phi^{i})\,\pi_{\beta}+...\,
. Therefore, for open algebras and algebras with higher-structure functions, whose
S^{\rm min}
contains second and higher powers of antifields,
\pi_{\alpha},\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
dependence of
S_{\varPsi}^{\rm min}
becomes more than quadratic, which does not allow us to integrate them out via Gaussian integration.

After the gauge fixing (
2.9
),
\varPhi^{*}\to\delta\varPsi/\delta\varPhi
, with the gauge fermion (
2.17
) one gets the nonminimal gauge-fixed action (
2.9
) which according to (
2.15
) is
S_{\varPsi}=S_{\varPsi}^{\rm min}+S_{\varPsi}^{\rm aux}
with

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptS^{\rm min}_{\varPsi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;S+\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}\big(X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\beta}\big)\mathcal{C}^{\beta}\!+\!\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\big(\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\alpha}\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\big)\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}\!+...,

(2.19)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptS^{\rm aux}_{\varPsi}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\;\tfrac{1}{2}\pi_{\alpha}\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}\pi_{\beta}+\pi_{\alpha}X^{\alpha}+\pi_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}

(2.20)

\displaystyle+\pi_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\alpha}\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}+\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}},

where
S=S[\,\phi\,]
is the action of the initial gauge theory and dots hide terms more then quadratic in ghost fields with nonzero ghost numbers.
2
2

2

Terms explicated in (
2.19
) and (
2.20
) are sufficient in the one-loop approximation. The gauge-fixed action with several higher-order terms reads

S^{\rm min}_{\varPsi}[\,\varPhi\,]\,=\,S[\,\phi\,]\,+\,\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}\big(X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\beta}\big)\mathcal{C}^{\beta}+\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\big(\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\alpha}\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\big)\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}+\,\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\big(\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\alpha,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\beta}+\tfrac{1}{2}\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\alpha}C_{\beta\gamma}^{\alpha}\big)\mathcal{C}^{\gamma}\mathcal{C}^{\beta}+\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\beta}\big(\tfrac{1}{2}X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}X^{\alpha}_{,{j}}B_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}^{{j}{i}}\big)\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}+\ldots\,\,.

We introduce the combination

X^{\alpha}\,\equiv\,\chi^{\alpha}(\phi)+\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}(\phi)\,\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}

(2.21)

which depends on fields
\phi^{i},\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
with zero ghost number.
All the dependence on
\mathcal{C}^{\prime}
in the gauge-fixed action
S_{\varPsi}
is now hidden within
X^{\alpha}
and its variational derivative

\displaystyle X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\,\equiv\,\delta X^{\alpha}/\delta\phi^{i}\,=\,\chi^{\alpha}_{\,,{i}}+\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}},{i}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}.

(2.22)

Reduction of Lagrange multiplier fields

For
\phi
-independent matrices
\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}
and
\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
— the case which we consider in what follows — Gaussian integration over the fields
\pi_{\alpha},\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}

in the generating functional (
2.10
) gives

Z=\int D\varPhi_{\rm red}\;\big(\!\det\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}\big)^{-1/2}\;{\det}\,\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\;e^{\,i\,S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}[\,\varPhi_{\rm red}]},

(2.23)

where
\varPhi_{\rm red}
is the reduced nonminimal set of fields
\varPhi_{\rm red}=(\phi^{i},\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}},\mathcal{C}^{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}})
, cf. Eq. (
II.1
), and
S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}[\,\varPhi_{\rm red}]
is the corresponding Faddeev-Popov reduced gauge-fixed action

\displaystyle S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}[\,\varPhi_{\rm red}]\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,S\,-\tfrac{1}{2}X^{\alpha}{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}

(2.24)

\displaystyle\,+\,\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}\big(X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\beta}-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,\rho^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}\big)\mathcal{C}^{\beta}

\displaystyle+\,\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\big(\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\alpha}\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\big)\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}+...\,.\;\;\;\;\;

Here we have introduced the matrices
{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}
,
{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
inverse respectively to
\varkappa^{\beta\alpha}
,
\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
. Dots denote terms which are more than quadratic in ghost fields
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
. Such terms are irrelevant in the one-loop approximation.

The obtained reduced action is nondegenerate in the sense that its Hessian at the stationary points of the classical BV action represents the nondegenerate operator

\mathrm{sdet}\,{\frac{\delta^{(l)}\delta^{(r)}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}}{\delta\varPhi_{\rm red}\,\delta\varPhi_{\rm red}}}\Bigg|_{\,\delta S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}/\delta\varPhi_{\rm red}=0}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\neq 0

(2.25)

for the relevant set of boundary conditions. This property is inherited from the nondegeneracy of the unreduced gauge-fixed action
S_{\varPsi}[\,\varPhi\,]
(with appropriately chosen gauge fermion), in which auxiliary nondynamical variables were integrated out (being expressed from their own equations of motion). For nondegenerate gauge-fixing matrices
\varkappa^{\beta\alpha}
,
\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
fields
\pi_{\alpha},\pi_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\pi^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
form the set of such auxiliary variables.

Now we will analyze nondegeneracy in various sectors of the reduced configuration space.

II.2
Stationary point of the gauge-fixed master action

The simplest is the ghost sector consisting of the fields
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
(the field
\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
with a zero ghost number does not belong to this sector even though it is usually called the extraghost
Batalin:1983ggl
— rather it belongs to zero ghost number sector where it plays a special role in gauge-fixing procedure for
\phi^{i}
). The variational equations for the fields
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha},\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
obviously make them vanishing under zero boundary conditions

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}=0,

(2.26)

provided the kernels of their bilinear forms in gauge-fixed action (
2.24
) represent invertible operators having well defined Green’s functions

\displaystyle F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}=X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\beta}-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta},\quad\det F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}\neq 0,\quad

(2.27)

\displaystyle F^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\alpha}\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}},\quad\det F^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\neq 0.

(2.28)

The interpretation of these operators and their properties is obvious. The first term of the ghost operator
F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}
is degenerate because of the reducibility of gauge generators, so the conventional Faddeev-Popov ghosts
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}
themselves become gauge fields with the local symmetry induced by the reducibility generators
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
. The second term of
F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}
in (
2.27
) plays the role of gauge breaking term for this symmetry, and its effect is the invertibility of
F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}
. Correspondingly,
F^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
is the Faddeev-Popov operator of new ghosts
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
for original ghosts
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}
treated as gauge fields, that is why the fields
\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
bear the name of ghosts for ghosts.

The situation is trickier in the zero ghost number sector of fields
\phi^{i},\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
. Their equations of motion read

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}}{\delta\phi^{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{,{i}}-X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}=0,

(2.29)

\displaystyle\frac{\delta S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}}{\delta\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}=0.

(2.30)

Contracting them respectively with
\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\gamma}
and
{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\gamma}
and subtracting from one another we obtain, on account of the Noether identity
S_{,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\gamma}=0
, the following on-shell relation:

\Big(X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\gamma}-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\gamma}\Big){\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}\;=\;F^{\alpha}_{\;\gamma}{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}\,=\,0,

(2.31)

which allows one in view of invertibility of
F^{\alpha}_{\;\gamma}
and
{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}
to rewrite the equations of motion in the zero ghost number sector as

\displaystyle S_{,{i}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle 0,

(2.32)

\displaystyle X^{\alpha}\!

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\chi^{\alpha}\!+\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,=\,0.

(2.33)

This can be interpreted as equations of motion for
\phi^{i}
supplied by the set of gauge conditions
X^{\alpha}=0
. The latter look overcomplete because the number of independent equations of motion
S_{,{i}}=0
on
n
variables
\phi^{i}
is
n-m_{0}+m_{1}
, whereas the number of gauges is
m_{0}
. The
m_{1}
mismatch is, however, corrected by the
m_{1}
extra ghost fields
\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
. This goes as follows.

Contracting the rank
m_{0}
nondegenerate operator
F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}
with the rank
m_{1}
full-rank generator
\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}
one gets the full-rank condition,

\rank{(\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}})}=m_{1}
,
which due to nondegeneracy of
F^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}=\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}
and
{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
implies the full-rank conditions
\rank{\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}}=m_{1}
,

\rank{\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}}=m_{1}
. This, in particular, guarantees that all
\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
are expressible in terms of
\phi^{i}
from the equations
X^{\alpha}=0
.

By introducing an arbitrary matrix
{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\alpha}(\phi^{i})
such that
\det{({s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}})}\neq 0
one can express the solution for
\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
in terms of
\phi^{i}
from Eq. (
2.33
)

\mathcal{C}^{\prime\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,=\,-({s}\sigma)^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{\,\beta}\,\chi^{\beta},

(2.34)

where
({s}\sigma)^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
is the inverse of
(s\sigma)^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}={s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\gamma}\sigma^{\gamma}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
. On the substitution of this solution in (
2.33
) the rest of relations constitute
m_{0}-m_{1}
independent gauge conditions

\Big(\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}({s}\sigma)^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}\,}{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}\Big)\chi^{\beta}\,=\,0,

(2.35)

on
n
original fields
\phi^{i}
as it should be. Indeed, what stands here as a matrix coefficient of
\chi^{\beta}
is the projector
T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(s,\sigma)
in the space of gauge indices having
m_{1}
right zero eigenvalue eigenvectors
\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
and
m_{1}
left zero covectors
s^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\;\alpha}
,

\displaystyle T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(s,\sigma)\,=\,\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}({s}\sigma)^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta},

(2.36)

\displaystyle T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(s,\sigma)\,\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,=\,0,\;\quad\;s^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\;\alpha}T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(s,\sigma)\,=\,0,

\displaystyle T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(s,\sigma)\,T^{\beta}_{\>\gamma}(s,\sigma)\,=\,T^{\alpha}_{\>\gamma}(s,\sigma),

\displaystyle\rank T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(s,\sigma)\,=\,m_{0}-m_{1},

(2.37)

which explains why Eq. (
2.35
) comprises
m_{0}-m_{1}
gauge conditions rather than
m_{0}
conditions. It is remarkable that despite the presence of an auxiliary element — an arbitrary matrix
{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}
of rank
m_{1}
— these gauge conditions and expressions for extraghosts (
2.34
) are
s
-independent. This easily follows on shell from the variational equation

\delta_{{s}}\big(\,({s}\sigma)^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}\chi^{\beta}\,\big)\,=\,({s}\sigma)^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\,\;\;\;\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}\,(\delta{{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}_{\,\gamma}})\,T^{\gamma}_{\>\beta}(s,\sigma)\,\chi^{\beta}\,=\,0.

(2.38)

Finally, consider the on-shell Hessian of the action in the zero ghost number sector of fields
\varPhi_{0}=(\phi^{i}
,
\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}})
which is actually the Hessian of
S[\,\phi\,]-\tfrac{1}{2}X^{\alpha}{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}
. Bearing in mind the on-shell value of
X^{\alpha}=0
we get the block-matrix operator

\displaystyle\left.\frac{\delta^{2}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}}{\delta\varPhi_{0}\,\delta\varPhi_{0}}\right|_{\,\delta S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}/\delta\varPhi_{\rm red}=0}

\displaystyle\qquad=\,\left[\begin{array}[]{cc}S_{,{i}{j}}{-}X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}_{,{j}}&\,\,-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}_{,{i}}\\
-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}_{,{i}}&\,\,-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\end{array}\right].

In view of invertibility of
\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}
one can introduce
the nondegenerate
m_{1}\times m_{1}
operator
\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
and its inverse

\displaystyle\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}},\;\quad\;{\kappa}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\equiv\big(\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\big)^{-1}.

(2.42)

Then one can factorize the determinant of (
II.2
) as the product of determinants of two operators

\left.\det\,\frac{\delta^{2}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}}{\delta\varPhi_{0}\,\delta\varPhi_{0}}\right|_{\,\delta S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}/\delta\varPhi_{\rm red}=0}\!\!\!\!=\;\det F_{ij}\,\det{\big({-}\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\big)},

(2.43)

where the new operator
F_{ij}
is obviously the gauge-fixed inverse propagator of fields
\phi^{i}

\displaystyle F_{{i}{j}}\,\equiv\,S_{,{i}{j}}-X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\,\varPi_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}_{,{j}},

(2.44)

\displaystyle\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\,\equiv\,{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}-{\varkappa}_{\alpha\gamma}\sigma^{\gamma}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}{\kappa}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\sigma^{{\,\delta}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}{\varkappa}_{{\,\delta}\beta},

(2.45)

in which the gauge-fixing term is built in terms of the gauge matrices
X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}
and the gauge-fixing matrix
\varPi_{\alpha\beta}
. The latter, however, does not coincide with the original matrix
\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}
in the gauge fermion (
2.17
), but rather converted to the projector form with the following properties:

\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\varPi_{\alpha\beta}=\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=0,\;\quad\;\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\varkappa^{\beta\gamma}\varPi_{\gamma{\,\delta}}=\varPi_{\alpha{\,\delta}}.

(2.46)

This projection is fully consistent with the fact that the rank
n-m_{0}+m_{1}
of
S_{,ij}
should be raised up to
n
by adding nonzero eigenvalues not in the
m_{0}
-dimensional subspace, but in the
(m_{0}-m_{1})
-dimensional one. In particular, with the projector (
2.45
) one can rewrite on-shell gauge conditions (
2.35
) on fields
\phi^{i}
as
\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\chi^{\beta}=0
. The equivalence of this to (
2.35
) is established by choosing
{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\beta}=m^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}
and noting that the projector
\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}({s}\sigma)^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}{s}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}
does not depend on a nondegenerate matrix factor
m^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
.

II.3
One-loop contribution to the generating functional

From (
2.23
) the one-loop contribution to the generating functional (its preexponential factor) reads

Z^{1-\rm loop}=\,\frac{\det\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}}{\big(\!\det\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}\big)^{1/2}}\,\left(\mathrm{sdet}\,\frac{\delta^{(l)}\delta^{(r)}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}}{\delta\varPhi_{\rm red}\,\delta\varPhi_{\rm red}}\,\right)^{-1/2}

(2.47)

where the Hessian in the sector of reduced fields has in virtue of (
2.24
) the following block matrix structure

\frac{\delta^{(l)}\delta^{(r)}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}}{\delta\varPhi_{\rm red}\,\delta\varPhi_{\rm red}}\,=\,\left[\begin{array}[]{c|c|c}\;{\displaystyle\frac{\delta^{2}S^{\scriptscriptstyle{FP}}}{\delta\varPhi_{0}\,\delta\varPhi_{0}}}&\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\;\,\,0\\
0&\;\,\,0\\
\end{array}&\;\begin{array}[]{cc}\;0&\;\,\,0\\
\;0&\;\,\,0\\
\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\;\,\,0\\
0&\;\,\,0\\
\end{array}&\hskip-2.0pt\begin{array}[]{cc}\;\,0&\;-F^{\beta}_{\alpha}\\
\;\;F^{\alpha}_{\beta}&0\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\;0&\;\,\,0\\
\;0&\;\,\,0\\
\end{array}\\
\hline\cr\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\;\,\,0\\
0&\;\,\,0\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}0&\;\,\,0\\
0&\;\,\,0\\
\end{array}&\begin{array}[]{cc}\;0&F^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\\
\;F^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}&0\\
\end{array}\\
\end{array}\right]\,.

(2.48)

Block in the left column and upper row here corresponds to the zero ghost number sector
\varPhi_{0}=(\phi^{i},\mathcal{C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}})
, the middle column and row block corresponds to the odd sector
(\mathcal{C}^{\beta},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\beta})
of gauge ghost and antighost fields, right bottom block corresponds to the even sector of ghost-for-ghost and antighost-for-ghosts fields
(\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}},\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}})
.

In virtue of the factorization property in the reduced fields block (
2.43
) the calculation of the full superdeterminant finally gives the one-loop contribution to the generating functional of the first-stage reducible gauge theory

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptZ^{1-\rm loop}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,\frac{\det\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}}{\big(\!\det\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}\big)^{1/2}}

(2.49)

\displaystyle\times\frac{\det F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}}{\big(\!\det F_{{i}{j}}\big)^{1/2}\big(\!\det\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\big)^{1/2}\det F^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}}.

Let us assemble together all numerous ingredients of this expression which were introduced above in the course of derivation of this formula. Here the inverse propagator of the original gauge field
F_{ij}
(
2.44
), the ghost operator
F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}
(
2.27
), and the ghosts-for-ghosts operator
F^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
(
2.28
) are, respectively,

\displaystyle F_{{i}{j}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle S_{,{i}{j}}-X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\varPi_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}_{,{j}},

(2.50)

\displaystyle F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\beta}-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta},

(2.51)

\displaystyle F^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\gamma}\mathcal{Z}^{\gamma}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}.

(2.52)

Gauge field and ghost operators (
2.50
), (
2.51
) are both gauge fixed with the aid of gauge conditions matrices
X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}
,
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
, and
\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}
and the gauge-fixing matrices
\varPi_{\alpha\beta}
and
{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
— kernels of bilinear terms in (
X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}
,
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
,
\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}
). Whereas these matrices of gauge conditions for ghosts
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
and
\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}
are a part of the originally chosen gauge fermion (
2.17
), a similar matrix in the gauge fields sector is a special projector
\varPi_{\alpha\beta}
(
2.45
),
\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\varkappa^{\beta\gamma}\varPi_{\gamma{\,\delta}}=\varPi_{\alpha{\,\delta}}
(
2.46
), on the direction in the space of gauge indices orthogonal to the “vielbein”
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
,
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\varPi_{\alpha\beta}=\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=0
.
This type of “orthogonality” is determined with respect to the symmetric metric
\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}
, which is inverse to
\varkappa^{\beta\alpha}
originally introduced in the gauge fermion (
2.17
). The projection of this metric onto the space of reducible generator indices
{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
with the aid of the vielbein
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
gives rise to the gauge-fixing matrix
\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
in the space of these indices. The determinants of all gauge-fixing matrices
\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}
,
\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
and
\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
appropriately enter the final algorithm for the one-loop generating functional (
2.49
).

Ranks of all the above gauge matrices are maximal and determined by the range of their indices, and the main criterion of their choice is the invertibility of the full set of gauge and ghost operators (
2.50
)-(
2.52
).

The final important comment is the definition of the on-shell condition for the obtained algorithm (
2.49
). All ghosts, ghosts for ghosts and their antighost fields are zero on shell,
\mathcal{C}^{\alpha}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{\alpha}=\mathcal{C}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}=\bar{\mathcal{C}}_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}=0
(
2.26
). The exception is the only “non-classical” field
\mathcal{C}^{\prime\,a}
which has a zero ghost number, though originally it was called the extraghost
Batalin:1983ggl
. On shell it is generically nonvanishing and is given by the expression (
2.34
), so that the gauge conditions matrix equals

X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\,=\,\chi^{\alpha}_{,{i}}-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}},{i}}\,({s}\sigma)^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{\,\beta}\,\chi^{\beta}

(2.53)

and coincides with
\chi^{\alpha}_{,i}
only for
\phi
-independent
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}
or vanishing on shell
{s}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{\,\beta}\chi^{\beta}
. For the original gauge field
\phi^{i}
on-shell restriction and gauge conditions (
2.32
)-(
2.33
) equivalently read
S_{,{i}}=0
,
\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\chi^{\beta}=0
(note that the original gauge functions
\chi^{\alpha}(\phi)
introduced in the gauge fermion (
2.17
) does not necessarily vanish — only their projection vanishes on shell).

II.4
Ward identities and gauge independence of the effective action

The one-loop effective action corresponding to (
2.49
) reads

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pti\varGamma^{1-\rm loop}{}\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}{\ln{F_{{i}{j}}}}+\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}{\ln{F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}}}-\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}{\ln{F^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}}}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\qquad\qquad\quad-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}{\ln\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}}-\tfrac{1}{2}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}{\ln{\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}}}+\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}{\ln{\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}}}\;\;

(2.54)

and includes gauge field, ghost, ghost-for-ghost contributions and the contribution of three gauge-fixing matrices. On shell this expression should not depend on all gauge-fixing entities
\chi^{\alpha}(\phi)
,
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}(\phi)
,
\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,\alpha}(\phi)
,
\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}
,
\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
as it is dictated by the general BV theory. It is worth checking this property and revealing the details of the perturbative mechanism of such gauge independence. In the one-loop approximation this mechanism is based on the on-shell Ward identities for all tree-level propagators of the theory:
F^{-1\,{i}{j}}
,
F^{-1\,\alpha}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\beta}
and
F^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
.

Ward identity for the Green’s function of the gauge field operator
F_{ij}
follows from the sequence of on-shell relations

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\mathcal{R}^{{i}}_{\hskip 0.5pt\gamma}\,=\,F^{-1\,{i}{j}}F_{{j}{k}}\mathcal{R}^{k}_{\gamma}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-\,F^{-1\,{i}{j}}X^{\alpha}_{,{j}}\varPi_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}_{\,,{k}}\mathcal{R}^{k}_{\gamma}

(2.55)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\,-\,F^{-1\,{i}{j}}X^{\alpha}_{,{j}}\varPi_{\alpha\beta}F^{\beta}_{\;\gamma},

where we used the fact that on shell
\mathcal{R}^{k}_{\gamma}
is a zero vector of
S_{,{j}{k}}
since
S_{,{j}{k}}\mathcal{R}^{j}_{\gamma}=-S_{,{j}}\mathcal{R}^{j}_{\gamma,{k}}
, and
\varPi_{\alpha\beta}X^{\beta}_{,{k}}\mathcal{R}^{k}_{\gamma}=\varPi_{\alpha\beta}F^{\beta}_{\;\gamma}
in view of
\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=0
. Thus, contracting this relation with
F^{-1\,\gamma}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;{\,\delta}}
, we get on shell

\displaystyle F^{-1\,ij}X^{\alpha}_{,{j}}\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\,\big|_{\,S_{,{i}}=0}=-\mathcal{R}^{{i}}_{\gamma}F^{-1\,\gamma}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\beta},

(2.56)

whence it follows the effective action independence on the choice of gauge matrix
X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}
and the gauge condition
\chi^{\alpha}(\phi)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pti\,\delta_{\chi}\varGamma^{\rm 1-loop}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle i\,\delta_{X}\varGamma^{\rm 1-loop}

(2.57)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=\big(F^{-1\,{i}{j}}X^{\alpha}_{,{i}}\varPi_{\alpha\beta}+\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\alpha}F^{-1\,\alpha}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\beta}\big)\,\delta X^{\beta}_{,{i}}\,=\,0.

Similarly, from the relation
{\mathcal{Z}}^{\gamma}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}=F^{-1\,\gamma}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\alpha}F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta\,}\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}=-F^{-1\,\gamma}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\alpha}\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\,}{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}F^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}

it follows that the Ward identity relating the ghost and ghosts-for-ghosts propagators is

\displaystyle F^{-1\,\gamma}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\alpha}\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\,}{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,\big|_{\,S_{,{i}}=0}=\,-\,{\mathcal{Z}}^{\gamma}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}F^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}},

(2.58)

and the effective action turns out to be on-shell independent of
\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{\,\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pti\,\delta_{\omega}\varGamma^{\rm 1-loop}

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\qquad=-\big(F^{-1\,\beta}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\alpha}\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}+\mathcal{Z}^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,F^{-1\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\big)\,\delta\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{\,\beta}\,=\,0.

(2.59)

The dependence of
\varGamma^{\rm 1-loop}
on
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
involves three terms of (
II.4
) the sum of which also turns out to be zero on shell

\displaystyle i\,\delta_{\sigma}\varGamma^{\rm 1-loop}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\delta_{\sigma}{\rm Tr}\,\ln F_{ij}+\delta_{\sigma}{\rm Tr}\ln F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}

(2.60)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}\,\delta_{\sigma}{\rm Tr}\ln\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\;=\;0.

To prove this one should use in the variation of the operator (
2.50
) the variation of the projector
\varPi_{\alpha\beta}
,
\delta_{\sigma}\varPi_{\alpha\beta}=-2\,\delta\sigma^{\gamma}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,\kappa^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,\varPi_{\gamma(\alpha}\,\varkappa_{\beta){\,\delta}}\,\sigma^{\,\delta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
, the variation of
\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
,
\delta_{\sigma}\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=2\,\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}\,\sigma^{\beta}_{({{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\delta\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}})}
, and the corollaries of (
2.56
) and (
2.58
),

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptX^{\gamma}_{\,,{i}}\,F^{-1\,{i}{j}}X^{\beta}_{\,,{j}}\,\varPi_{\beta\alpha}\,\big|_{\,S_{,{i}}=0}=-X^{\gamma}_{\,,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\beta}\,F^{-1\,\beta}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\alpha},

(2.61)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636ptX^{\gamma}_{\,,{i}}\mathcal{R}^{i}_{\beta}\,F^{-1\,\beta}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\alpha}=\big(F^{\gamma}_{\;\beta}+\sigma^{\gamma}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\,}{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}\,}\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}\big)\,F^{-1\,\beta}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\alpha}

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\qquad\qquad\qquad=\delta^{\gamma}_{\,\alpha}+\,\sigma^{\gamma}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\,}{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}\,}\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta\,}F^{-1\,\beta}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\alpha},

(2.62)

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{\,\beta\,}F^{-1\,\beta}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\,\alpha}\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,\big|_{\,S_{,{i}}=0}=-\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}.

(2.63)

The
\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}
-variation of the effective action also vanishes on shell because of the variations
\delta_{\varkappa}\varPi_{\alpha\beta}=-\varPi_{\alpha{\,\delta}}\delta\varkappa^{{\,\delta}\gamma}\varPi_{\gamma\beta}
,
\delta_{\!\varkappa}\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\delta{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
, Eq. (
2.62
) and its corollary
\varPi_{\alpha\gamma}X^{\gamma}_{\,,{i}}F^{-1\,{i}{j}}X^{{\,\delta}}_{\,,{j}}\varPi_{{\,\delta}\beta}=-\varPi_{\alpha\beta}
(which in its turn is provided by the orthogonality relation
\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=0
),

\displaystyle i\,\delta_{\varkappa}\varGamma^{\rm 1-loop}

\displaystyle\;=

\displaystyle\;-\tfrac{1}{2}F^{-1\,{j}{i}}\delta_{\!\varkappa}F_{{i}{j}}-\tfrac{1}{2}{\kappa}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\delta_{\!\varkappa}\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}

(2.64)

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{2}{\varkappa}_{\beta\alpha}\delta\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}\;=\;0.

Finally, on-shell independence of the gauge-fixing matrix
\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
follows by direct variation and the use of the corollary (
2.63
) of the Ward identity (
2.58
) for the ghost propagator,

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pti\,\delta_{\rho}\varGamma^{\rm 1-loop}

(2.65)

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt=-\big(\omega^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}\,F^{-1\,\beta}_{\;\;\;\;\;\;\alpha}\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}+\rho^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\big)\,\delta{{\rho^{-1}}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}}\;=\;0.

III
Reducible gauge structure of restricted gauge theories

Reducible structure of a gauge theory can be induced by the procedure of
restriction
of the originally irreducible gauge theory. To see this consider a generic gauge theory with the action
\hat{S}[\,\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}\,]
subject to a closed gauge algebra of
irreducible
generators
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}
,

\displaystyle\hat{S}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}=0,\;\quad\;\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha,{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}_{\beta}-\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\beta,{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}_{\alpha}=\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\gamma}\hat{C}^{\gamma}_{\alpha\beta}.

(3.1)

Irreducibility of the generators
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}
implies that the rank of their matrices coincides with the range of the indices
\alpha
which in its turn is lower than the range of the indices
{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}
enumerating the original gauge fields
\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}
,
\rank{\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}}={\rm range}\>\alpha=m_{0}<\hat{n}={\rm range}\>{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}
. We will call such a theory the
parental
one.

Restricted
gauge theory, originating from the parental one, is the theory whose configuration space variables are kinematically constrained by the equations

\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}(\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}})\,=\,0,

(3.2)

where we will consider the functions
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}(\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}})
to be functionally independent, that is characterized by the full-rank condition of their gradient matrix,

\rank{\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}}\,=\,m_{1}\,\equiv\,{\rm range}\>{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}.

(3.3)

For simplicity we assume that these functions are either ultralocal (algebraic) in spacetime or the surface of these constraint functions can be parametrized in terms of local independent fields which will be denoted in what follows by
\phi^{i}
.

III.1
Two representations of a restricted theory

Such a restricted theory can be described in two equivalent ways. One way is to represent it in terms of the Lagrange multipliers action

S^{\lambda}[\,\varphi,\lambda\,]\,=\,\hat{S}[\,\varphi\,]-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}(\varphi),

(3.4)

whose classical equations of motion obtained by varying both its gauge fields
\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}
and Lagrange multipliers
\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
read

\hat{S}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}-\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\,\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}=0,\;\quad\;\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}=0.

(3.5)

Another representation is the
reduced
theory, when one solves first the constraints (
3.2
) with respect to
\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}
as functions of the reduced set of fields
\phi^{i}
and formulates the theory in terms of the
reduced
action
S^{\rm red}[\,\phi\,]
. The latter is obtained by substituting in the parental theory action the functions
e^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}(\phi)
of embedding the
\phi^{i}
-subspace into the space of original gauge fields
\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}
,

\displaystyle\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}=e^{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}(\phi),\;\quad\;\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\big(e^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}(\phi)\big)\equiv 0,

(3.6)

\displaystyle S^{\rm red}[\,\phi\,]=\hat{S}[\,e(\phi)\,].

(3.7)

The equations of motion in both formulations are obviously equivalent
3
3

3

Classical equivalence of two theories can be defined by equivalence of spaces of solutions of their equations of motion. In particular it can be established by the local correspondence (
3.8
) and the statement that reduced fields do not carry additional degrees of freedom. This statement means that the above assumptions on
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}(\varphi)
imply the existence of such reducibility, that is the possibility of excluding auxiliary variables in terms of the reduced ones.
because the contraction of the first of Eqs. (
3.5
) results in
