\sigma^{p}_{\,\alpha}\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,q}=\delta^{p}_{\,q},\,\quad\,\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,p}\sigma^{p}_{\,\beta}=\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}-\varPi^{\alpha}_{\beta},\,\quad\,\varPi^{\alpha}_{\beta}=\varkappa^{\alpha\gamma}\varPi_{\gamma\beta}

(4.37)

On the other hand, inclusion of delta function of the subset of gauge conditions (or partial gauge fixing) can be interpreted as quantization of the
restricted
gauge theory with partial gauge-fixing conditions
\chi^{p}
playing the role of restriction constraints
\theta^{p}
. According to our derivation above, this restricted gauge theory has reducible gauge generators
\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}=\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\beta}T^{\beta}_{\>\alpha}(Q,k)
built with the aid of the operator
Q^{p}_{\,\alpha}=\chi^{p}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}
and some set of vectors
k^{\alpha}_{\,p}
, so that its path integral over
reduced
configuration space of
\phi^{i}
should read

Z_{\rm partial\;gf}=\int D\phi\,\,M^{\rm red}_{(\chi,\varkappa,\sigma,\omega,k,\rho)}\,e^{\,i\,S^{\rm red}[\,\phi\,]},

(4.38)

where
S^{\rm red}[\,\phi\,]=\hat{S}[\,\varphi\,]\,|_{\,\chi^{p}=0}
and the gauge-fixing integration measure according to the reducible gauge theory algorithm (
2.49
) looks like (we distinguish the partial gauge fixing case from the restricted theory case by replacing the indices
a,b,...
with the indices
p,q,...
from the second part of Latin alphabet)
13
13

13

The reason for that which will be clarified below is that the set of restriction conditions
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
generically contains a gauge-fixing subset, which will be labeled by indices
p,q,...
of range
m_{1}-m_{2}
, and the remaining conditions of range
m_{2}
which restrict gauge invariants of the parental theory.

\displaystyle M^{\rm red}_{(\chi,\varkappa,\sigma,\omega,k,\rho)}=\frac{\det F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}\,\det\rho^{p}_{\,q}}{\det F^{p}_{\>q}}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\quad\quad\;\;\times\frac{\big(\!\det\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}\big)^{1/2}}{\big(\!\det\kappa_{pq}\big)^{1/2}}\;e^{-\frac{i}{2}\chi^{\alpha}\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\chi^{\beta}},\;\;\;

(4.39)

\displaystyle F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}\,=\,\hat{Q}^{\alpha}_{\,\gamma}\,T^{\gamma}_{\>\beta}(Q,k)-\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,p}\rho^{-1\,p}_{\;\;\;\;\;q}\omega^{q}_{\,\beta},

(4.40)

\displaystyle F^{p}_{\>q}\,=\,(\omega\mathcal{Z})^{p}_{q}\,=\,(\omega k)^{p}_{r}\,(Qk)^{-1\,r}_{\;\;\;\;\;q},

(4.41)

\displaystyle\,Q\,\equiv\,Q^{p}_{\,\alpha}\,=\,\sigma^{p}_{\,\beta}\,\hat{Q}^{\beta}_{\,\alpha}.

(4.42)

Here
Q^{p}_{\,\alpha}
is in fact a projected (from the left) Faddeev-Popov operator
\hat{Q}^{\beta}_{\,\alpha}
of the
parental
theory,
T^{\gamma}_{\>\beta}(Q,k)
— the corresponding projector (
3.22
) with kernels
Q^{p}_{\,\alpha}
and
k^{\alpha}_{\,q}
,
F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}
is the gauge-fixed ghost operator,
F^{p}_{\>q}
is the ghosts-for-ghosts operator and first-stage reducibility generators
\mathcal{Z}^{\alpha}_{\,p}
are normalized according to (
4.27
).

Remarkable property proven in Appendix
C
is the determinant relation which is valid for operators defined by Eqs. (
4.34
) and (
4.41
),

\det\hat{Q}^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}\,=\,\frac{\det F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}\,\det\rho^{p}_{\,q}}{\det F^{p}_{\>q}}.

(4.43)

It allows one to express the combined measure (
4.35
) in terms of the restricted theory measure

\hat{M}^{\rm delta}_{(\chi,\varkappa,\sigma)}\,=\,M^{\rm red}_{(\chi,\varkappa,\sigma,\omega,k,\rho)}\,\delta(\sigma^{p}_{\,\alpha}\chi^{\alpha}),

(4.44)

whence one has

Z_{\rm partial\;gf}=\int D\varphi\,\,\hat{M}^{\rm delta}_{(\chi,\varkappa,\sigma)}\,e^{\,i\,\hat{S}[\,\varphi\,]}=\,Z_{\rm parental},

(4.45)

where the last equality follows from (
4.31
). This relation shows that the restricted theory with
full-rank
operator
Q^{p}_{\,\alpha}
is nothing but a
partial gauge fixing
concept — the parental and restricted theories which are physically equivalent at the classical level remain equivalent at the quantum level.

The BFV canonical prescription of normalization for gauge generators in the Lagrangian BV approach for the parental theory with the irreducible gauge structure thus imply that correctly normalized reducible “projected” gauge generators
\mathcal{R}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}=\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\beta}T^{\beta}_{\>\alpha}(Q,k)
of the restricted (here, partially gauge-fixed) formalism just should be constructed by projecting
canonically normalized
parental gauge generators
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\beta}
. Note that this result also confirms the normalization (
4.27
) of reducibility generators derived above from another (projector parameter independence) principle.

Let us go over from the case of partial gauge fixing to generic restricted theory physically inequivalent to the parental one. Its path integral in reduced space representation is given by the analog of Eq. (
4.38
) with the set of restriction conditions
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
instead of
\chi^{p}
, whose operator
Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}
is rank deficient,
\rank Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}=m_{1}-m_{2}<{\rm range}\>{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}=m_{1}
,

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274ptZ_{\rm restricted}

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\int D\phi\,M^{\rm red}_{(\chi,\varkappa,\sigma,\omega,k,\rho)}\,e^{\,i\,S^{\rm red}[\,\phi\,]}

(4.46)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int D\varphi\,M^{\rm red}_{(\chi,\varkappa,\sigma,\omega,k,\rho)}\,\delta(\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}})\,e^{\,i\,\hat{S}[\,\varphi\,]}.

The transition to integration over the parental theory configuration space,
D\phi\,(\det G_{{i}{j}})^{1/2}=D\varphi\,(\det G_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}})^{1/2}\,(\det G^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}})^{1/2}\,\delta(\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}})
, is written here modulo local measure factors which we will disregard in what follows. The set of functions
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
according to the rank of
Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}
can be split into the set of gauge-invariant functions
\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}
,
{\rm range}\>{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}=m_{2}
, and the set
\theta^{p}
,
{\rm range}\>p=m_{1}-m_{2}
, cf. Eqs. (
3.12
)–(
3.13
),
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\to(\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}},\theta^{p})
so that

\delta(\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}})\,=\,\delta(\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}})\,\delta(\theta^{p})\,Y,

(4.47)

where
Y=\det\partial(\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}},\theta^{p})/\partial\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
. The functions
\theta^{p}
enumerated by letters from the second part of Latin alphabet play here the role conditions of partial gauge fixing,
\chi^{p}\equiv\theta^{p}
, while the invariants
\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}
, which are forced to vanish in the path integral, are responsible for inequivalence of the restricted and parental theories.

Now, let us choose in the measure
M^{\rm red}_{(\chi,\varkappa,\sigma,\omega,k,\rho)}
the full set of gauge conditions

\chi^{\alpha}\,=\,\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,p}\chi^{p}+\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,M}\chi^{M}

(4.48)

with
\chi^{p}
identified with
\theta^{p}
,
\chi^{p}=\theta^{p}
,
{\rm range}\;p=m_{1}-m_{2}
(cf. the footnote
12
and Eq. (
4.36
) which applies here in view of orthogonality
\sigma^{p}_{\,\alpha}\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,M}=0
). Let the rest of gauge conditions
\chi^{M}
,
{\rm range}\>M=m_{0}-m_{1}+m_{2}
, form any complementary set of gauges such that the total Faddeev-Popov operator
[\,Q^{p}_{\,\beta}\;Q^{M}_{\,\beta}\hskip 1.0pt]
is nondegenerate. Then, substituting the above relation (
4.47
) in (
4.46
) and using the relation (
4.44
) between the measures one has

\displaystyle Z_{\rm restricted}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int D\varphi\,\hat{M}^{\rm delta}_{(\chi,\varkappa,\sigma)}Y\,\delta(\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}})\,e^{\,i\,\hat{S}[\,\varphi\,]}

(4.49)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int D\varphi\,\hat{M}^{\rm delta}_{(\chi)}Y\,\delta(\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}})\,e^{\,i\,\hat{S}[\,\varphi\,]}.

Here, the last equality follows from the equality of measures Eq. (
4.31
) and the fact that

\det\!\big[\,Q^{p}_{\,\beta}\;Q^{M}_{\,\beta}\hskip 1.0pt\big]\delta(\chi^{p})\delta(\chi^{M})\,=\,\det\hat{Q}^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}\delta(\chi^{\alpha})\,\equiv\,M^{\rm delta}_{(\chi)}

(4.50)

in view of invariance of the Faddeev-Popov delta-function-type measure with respect to linear transformations of the basis of gauge conditions.

Equation (
4.49
) allows us to make the needed statement: as long as the quantum measure
M^{\rm delta}_{(\chi)}
can be derived from the canonical quantization with canonically normalized local generators
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}
, then the same choice of parental theory generators should be used in the construction of restricted theory.

Note that the last expression for
Z_{\rm restricted}
can be alternatively represented by expressing in terms of original special gauge conditions
\theta^{p}
and
\chi^{M}
and then using (
4.47
). This allows us to get rid of a potentially nonlocal factor
Y
and obtain the representation directly in terms of the restriction functions
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
and the complementary subset of gauge conditions,

\hskip-8.5359ptZ_{\rm restricted}=\int D\varphi\,\det\big[\,Q^{p}_{\,\beta}\,Q^{M}_{\,\beta}\,\big]\delta(\chi^{M\!})\,\delta(\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}})\,e^{\,i\,\hat{S}[\,\varphi\,]}.

(4.51)

It should be emphasized again that here the Faddeev-Popov operator
[\,Q^{p}_{\,\beta}\,\,\,Q^{M}_{\,\beta}\,]
is built with respect to partial gauge fixing subset of
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
and any complementary to it set of gauge conditions
\chi^{M}
. This recipe may be less suitable in concrete applications, because explicit disentangling the subset
\theta^{p}
from
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
may be involved, so the original form (
4.46
) should be more useful, and it was explicitly used above for the derivation of the one-loop generating functional (
4.19
).

IV.4
The difference between parental and restricted theories in the one-loop approximation

For a class of theories with the Jacobian
Y
independent of integration fields in (
4.49
) one can write down the representation for
Z_{\rm restricted}
and its one-loop order (such irrelevant
Y
will be omitted in the subsequent expressions in this section). In view of gauge invariance of
\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}
Eq. (
4.49
) implies on shell the usual Faddeev-Popov integral with
\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}
-fixed gauge and with the
gauge-invariant
insertion of
\delta(\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}})

Z_{\rm restricted}\,=\,\int D\varphi\,\hat{M}_{(\chi,\varkappa)}\,\delta(\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}})\,e^{\,i\,\hat{S}[\,\varphi\,]},

(4.52)

which in the one-loop order by the mechanism of the identical transformation of Eq. (
4.8
) goes over to a simple relation between the generating functionals of the restricted and parental theories

Z_{\rm restricted}^{\rm 1-loop}\,=\,\hat{Z}^{\rm 1-loop}\,\big(\!\det\varTheta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}\big)^{-1/2},

(4.53)

where
\hat{Z}^{\rm 1-loop}
is obviously the one-loop generating functional of the parental theory given in terms of its (hatted) gauge and ghost inverse propagators

\displaystyle\hat{Z}^{\rm 1-loop}=\frac{\det\hat{Q}^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}\,}{\big(\det\!\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}\big)^{1/2}\big(\!\det\hat{F}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\big)^{1/2}},

(4.54)

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\,=\,\hat{S}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}-\chi^{\alpha}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}\chi^{\beta}_{,{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}-\lambda_{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}

(4.55)

and

\varTheta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}\,\equiv\,\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\hat{F}^{-1\,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\theta^{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}_{,{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}

(4.56)

The operator (
4.56
) is analogous to (
4.9
), but it is acting in the space of indices
{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}
which enumerate gauge-invariant functions
\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}
disentangled from the full set of
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
. Note that it is defined in terms of the Green’s function of the gauge-fixed operator
\hat{F}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}
of the parental theory with a source
\lambda_{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}
(at gauge-invariant observable
\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}
). The presence of such source term in parental theory may be interpreted as going off shell and performing one-loop calculations on the family of backgrounds
\hat{S}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}=\lambda_{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}
, which (together with
\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}(\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}})=0
conditions) specify saddle points of (
4.52
). This is in accordance with the fact that solutions of these background equations cover all possible backgrounds of the restricted theory. To compare
Z_{\rm restricted}^{\rm 1-loop}
and
\hat{Z}^{\rm 1-loop}
in (
4.53
) these objects of course should be calculated on the same backgrounds.
14
14

14

According to rather generic assumptions on restriction conditions
\theta^{a}=0
(see discussion in Sec.
III.1
) the Lagrange multipliers
\lambda^{a}
, and thus
\lambda^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}
, are expressible in terms of the background fields. In direct analogy with (
3.17
) here
\lambda_{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}(\varphi)=\hat{S}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}(\varphi)\theta^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}(\varphi)
for
\theta^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}(\varphi)
being dual to
\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}
.

Another important observation confirming the consistency of the relation (
4.53
) is that the additional factor depending on the matrix
\varTheta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}
and this matrix itself are independent on shell of the choice of gauge,
\delta_{(\chi,\varkappa)}\varTheta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}=0
, which can be checked by using the Ward identities for
\hat{F}^{-1\,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}
derived above.

Finally, let us note on practical aspects regarding the structure of (
4.56
). Calculation of
\varTheta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}
based on nonlocal
\hat{F}^{-1\,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}
may be technically inconvenient. The transition to objects defined in terms of a local differential operator
\hat{F}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}
significantly simplifies calculations. When the dual biorthogonal basis
(e^{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}^{\prime}},\theta^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}})
satisfying
\theta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}e^{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}^{\prime}}=0
and
\theta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\theta^{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}_{{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}=\delta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}}_{\,{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}
, is “orthogonal” with respect to the operator
\hat{F}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}
in the sense that
e^{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}^{\prime}}\hat{F}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\theta^{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}_{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}}=0
, then it is easy to calculate operator
\varTheta_{{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}}
inverse to
\varTheta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}
:
\varTheta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}\varTheta_{{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}{\mathchoice{C}{C}{\scriptscriptstyle{C}}{C}}}=\delta^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}}_{\,{\mathchoice{C}{C}{\scriptscriptstyle{C}}{C}}}
. Under the above assumptions the determinant
(\det{\varTheta^{{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}}})^{-1/2}
in (
4.53
) can be replaced by
(\det\varTheta_{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}})^{1/2}
— the inverse operator acquiring simple form in terms of local
\hat{F}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}

\varTheta_{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}\,=\,\theta^{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}_{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}}\hat{F}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\theta^{{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}_{{\mathchoice{B}{B}{\scriptscriptstyle{B}}{B}}}.

(4.57)

V
Unimodular gravity theory

Application of the above formalism to unimodular gravity theory is straightforward. Its
parental
theory is Einstein general relativity with the action — the functional of the metric field

\displaystyle\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}\;\mapsto\;g_{\mu\nu}(x),\,\quad\,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}\;\mapsto\;(\mu\nu,x),

(5.1)

\displaystyle\hat{S}[\,\varphi\,]\;\mapsto\;S_{\rm E}[\,g_{\mu\nu}]=\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}(x)\,R(g_{\mu\nu}(x)),\qquad

(5.2)

where
g(x)\equiv-\det g_{\mu\nu}(x)
and
R(g_{\mu\nu}(x))
is the scalar curvature of this metric (for brevity we work in units with
16\pi G=1
). The arrow signs (
\,\mapsto\,
) signify in what follows the realization of condensed notations of previous sections in this concrete field model.

Einstein theory is invariant under local gauge transformations
\delta_{\xi}\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}=\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}_{\alpha}\xi^{\alpha}
— metric diffeomorphisms generated by the vector field
\xi^{\alpha}
, which read in explicit notations
\delta_{\xi}g_{\mu\nu}=\nabla_{\mu}\xi_{\nu}+\nabla_{\nu}\xi_{\mu}
,
\xi^{\alpha}\,\;\mapsto\;\,\xi^{\alpha}(x)\equiv g^{\alpha\mu}\xi_{\mu}(x)
, so that the gauge generators
\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}
have the form

\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}\;\mapsto\;2g_{\alpha(\mu}\nabla_{\nu)}\delta(x,y),\,\quad\,I\;\mapsto\;(\mu\nu,x),\quad\alpha\;\mapsto\;(\alpha,y).

(5.3)

By default, derivatives act to the right on the first spacetime-point argument of delta functions.

Unimodular restriction of the theory (
5.2
) consists in the restriction to the subspace of metrics
\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}(x)=g_{\mu\nu}(x)/g^{1/4}(x)
with a unit determinant,
\textsl{g}(x)\equiv-\det\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}(x)=1
. We will not introduce a special notation for nine independent variables per spacetime point playing the role of
\phi^{i}
but just formulate the restriction constraints
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}(\varphi)
as

\displaystyle\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\theta^{x}\equiv\theta(x)=g^{1/2}(x)-1,\quad{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\;\mapsto\;x,\quad

(5.4)

\displaystyle\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\theta^{x,\>\mu\nu,y}=\tfrac{1}{2}g^{1/2}g^{\mu\nu}\delta(x,y),

(5.5)

\displaystyle\qquad{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\;\mapsto\;x,\quad{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}\;\mapsto\;(\mu\nu,y),

and the gauge-restriction operator (
3.11
) has the form

Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}\;\mapsto\;Q^{x}_{\alpha,y}=g^{1/2}\nabla_{\alpha}\delta(x,y)=\partial_{\alpha}\big(g^{1/2}(x)\delta(x,y)\big),

(5.6)

where
\nabla_{\alpha}
is a covariant derivative with Christoffel connection, here acting on a vector field.

Counterpart of restricted theory (
3.4
) — the Lagrange multiplier action of unimodular gravity, and its metric equations of motion read

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38092ptS^{\lambda}_{\rm E}[\,g_{\mu\nu},\lambda\,]\!=\!\int d^{4}x\big(\,g^{1/2}R(g_{\mu\nu}\!)-\lambda(g^{1/2}\!-\!1)\big),

(5.7)

\displaystyle\hskip-11.38092pt\frac{\delta S^{\lambda}_{\rm E}}{\delta g_{\mu\nu}}=-g^{1/2}\big(R^{\mu\nu}-\tfrac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}R+\tfrac{1}{2}\lambda g^{\mu\nu}\big)=0.

(5.8)

The latter resembles Einstein equations with the term mimicking cosmological constant: the Lagrange multiplier
\lambda(x)
on shell is indeed
constant
because according to (
3.10
) its nonzero part is a spacetime constant zero mode of the gauge-restriction operator (
5.6
),

\lambda_{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}=0\;\mapsto\;\int d^{4}x\,\lambda(x)\,Q^{x}_{\alpha,y}=-g^{1/2}\nabla_{\alpha}\lambda(y)=0,

(5.9)

where the covariant derivative is acting on scalar.
This result is obviously equivalent to contracting (
5.8
) with covariant derivative and using contracted Bianchi identity for the Einstein tensor. On the other hand, tracing this equation one finds
\lambda=R/2
, and the set of ten metric equations becomes linearly dependent which corresponds to Eqs. (
3.16
) and (
3.17
) of the general formalism of restricted gauge theories,

R^{\mu\nu}-\tfrac{1}{4}g^{\mu\nu}R=0,\,\quad\,\nabla_{\mu}R=0.

(5.10)

The
vacuum solution
of these equations is a generic Einstein space metric
\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}
,
R_{\mu\nu}=\varLambda\,\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}
,
\varLambda=\lambda/2={\rm const}
with a unit determinant
\textsl{g}\equiv-\det\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}=1
.

Thus, the left kernel of the operator
Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}
of dimensionality
m_{2}=1
spanned by the zero mode
Y^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,\;\mapsto\;\,Y_{x}=1
,
{\rm range}\>{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}=m_{2}=1
, is what physically distinguishes unimodular gravity theory from Einstein (or Einstein-Hilbert theory with a cosmological constant term) because it allows one to prescribe any constant value of
\lambda
from the initial conditions rather than postulate it as a fundamental constant in the Lagrangian of the theory. The relevant gauge-invariant physical degree of freedom constrained by the unimodular restriction according to Eqs. (
3.13
)–(
3.14
) above,

\theta^{A}=Y^{{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}=0\,\;\mapsto\;\,\bar{\theta}=\int d^{4}x\,(g^{1/2}(x)-1)=0,

(5.11)

is the full spacetime volume
\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}\,|_{\,\theta=0}=\int d^{4}x
which, of course,
a priori
is not completely specified in Einstein gravity.

The construction of projectors (
3.22
) with the aid of the matrix
k^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
satisfying the rank restriction conditions (
3.23
) suggests the following obvious choice which simultaneously with these conditions provides covariance with respect to spacetime diffeomorphisms,

\displaystyle k^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle k^{\alpha,x}_{\,y}=\nabla^{\alpha}\delta(x,y)=g^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\delta(x,y),

(5.12)

\displaystyle\qquad\alpha\;\mapsto\;(\alpha,x),\quad{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}\;\mapsto\;y,

\displaystyle(Qk)^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle(Qk)^{x}_{\,y}=g^{1/2}\Box\delta(x,y)=\partial_{\mu}\big(g^{1/2}g^{\mu\nu}\partial_{\mu}\delta(x,y)\big),\,

(5.13)

\displaystyle\qquad{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\;\mapsto\;x,\quad{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}\;\mapsto\;y,

which also provides covariance with respect to spacetime coordinate change. Here
\nabla^{\alpha}
is the covariant derivative with respect to the
dynamical
metric
g_{\alpha\beta}
and
\Box
is its covariant d’Alembertian,

\displaystyle\nabla^{\alpha}=g^{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\beta},\;\quad\;\Box=g^{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta},

(5.14)

which act here on the scalars (we use the standard definition of
\delta(x,y)
as the symmetric kernel of the scalar identity operator), though in what follows we reserve for them the same notation when they will be acting on general tensors and tensor densities. With such choice projector (
3.22
) reads

\displaystyle T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(Q,k)

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle T^{\alpha,x}_{\>\beta,y}=T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(\nabla)\delta(x,y),

(5.15)

\displaystyle\qquad T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(\nabla)\,=\,\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}-\nabla^{\alpha}\tfrac{1}{\Box}\nabla_{\beta}.

T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(\nabla)
is a projector on the subspace of spacetime transverse vectors, and it is nonlocal because it is defined in terms of the Green’s function of
\Box
operator, which is understood in the Moore-Penrose sense associated with the rank deficiency of operators (
5.6
), (
5.12
), and (
5.13
) — the number
m_{2}
of their spacetime constant zero modes being just
1
.

V.1
Gauge fixing and propagators

Now we go over to the construction of auxiliary elements of gauge-fixing procedure
\chi^{\alpha}
,
\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}
,
\sigma^{\alpha}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
,
\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}
and
\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
. To preserve covariance of the formalism we will use background covariant gauge conditions which for simplicity will be linear in the dynamical (quantum) metric field
\varphi^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}=g_{\mu\nu}
. The coefficient of
g_{\mu\nu}
in
\chi^{\alpha}
, that is
\chi^{\alpha}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}
, is
g_{\mu\nu}
-independent but explicitly depends on the
background
metric
\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}
— the one which on shell satisfies the equations of motion (
5.10
) and is subject to unimodularity restriction
\textsl{g}=1
. Background covariance of such gauge conditions implies that the choice of this coefficient should be such that
\chi^{\alpha}
is covariant with respect to simultaneous diffeomorphisms of both
g_{\mu\nu}
and
\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}
. Usually as such a gauge one uses the linearized de Donder or DeWitt gauge which is linear in
h_{\mu\nu}(x)=g_{\mu\nu}(x)-\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}(x)
— the quantum fluctuation of the metric on top of its background. The DeWitt gauge is
\chi^{\alpha}\;\mapsto\;\chi^{\alpha}(x)=\textsl{g}^{1/2}\textsl{g}^{\alpha\beta}(\nabla^{\mu}h_{\beta\mu}-\tfrac{1}{2}\textsl{g}^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\beta}h_{\mu\nu})
, where, as well as below, the covariant derivatives
\nabla_{\beta}
and
\nabla^{\mu}\equiv\textsl{g}^{\mu\nu}\nabla_{\nu}
are constructed in terms of the
background
metric. In the unimodular case, however, the trace part
\textsl{g}^{\mu\nu}h_{\mu\nu}
of the metric fluctuation is systematically projected out, so that it is worth using a simpler gauge

\displaystyle\chi^{\alpha}\;\mapsto\;\chi^{\alpha}(x)\!

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\!\textsl{g}^{1/2}\nabla^{\mu}h^{\alpha}_{\mu}(x)

(5.16)

\displaystyle\equiv

\displaystyle\!\textsl{g}^{1/2}\textsl{g}^{\mu\beta}\textsl{g}^{\alpha\nu}\nabla_{\!\beta}\big(g_{\mu\nu}(x)-\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}(x)\big).\quad\quad\;\;

For the same reasons of covariance the choice of gauge-fixing matrices
\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}
,
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
,
\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}
and
\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
is also obvious. Just like
\chi^{\alpha}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}
above we will construct them in terms of the background metric, part of them being directly related to the already introduced quantities

\displaystyle\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}\!

\displaystyle\,\mapsto\,

\displaystyle\varkappa^{\alpha,x\,\beta,y}\!=\textsl{g}^{1/2}\textsl{g}^{\alpha\beta}\delta(x,y),

(5.17)

\displaystyle\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,

\displaystyle\,\mapsto\,

\displaystyle\sigma^{\alpha,x}_{\,y}\!=-\textsl{g}^{1/2}\textsl{g}^{\alpha\beta}\nabla_{\beta}\delta(x,y)=-\textsl{g}^{1/2}\textsl{g}^{\alpha\beta}\partial_{\beta}\delta(x,y)

(5.18)

\displaystyle\qquad\;=-\textsl{g}^{1/2}(x)k^{\alpha,x}_{\,y}|_{\,g_{\mu\nu}\to\,\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}},

\displaystyle\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}

\displaystyle\,\mapsto\,

\displaystyle\omega^{x}_{\alpha,y}\!=\textsl{g}^{1/2}\nabla_{\alpha}\delta(x,y)=\partial_{\alpha}\big(\textsl{g}^{1/2}\delta(x,y)\big)

(5.19)

\displaystyle\qquad\;=Q^{x}_{\,\alpha,y}|_{\,g_{\mu\nu}\to\,\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}},

\displaystyle\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}

\displaystyle\,\mapsto\,

\displaystyle\rho^{x}_{\,y}\!=\textsl{g}^{1/2}\delta(x,y),

(5.20)

where in (
5.18
) covariant derivative acts on scalar, whereas in (
5.19
) covariant derivative acts on vector (forming a covariant divergence).

As the on-shell results do not depend on the choice of these quantities, they could have been chosen in terms of the quantum field
g_{\mu\nu}
, but this would have lead to the origin of extra terms involving functional derivatives
\varkappa^{\alpha\beta}_{\,,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}
,
\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}},{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}
, etc. Avoiding such terms essentially simplifies calculations and, particular, allows one to avoid explicit use of the extra ghost
{\cal C}^{\prime{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
entering (
2.21
), because in view of (
2.22
)

\displaystyle X^{\alpha}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}=\chi^{\alpha}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\,\;\mapsto\;\,\textsl{g}^{1/2}\textsl{g}^{\alpha(\mu}\nabla^{\nu)}\delta(x,y).

(5.21)

It should be emphasized that, as long as we restrict ourselves with the one-loop approximation, after all needed functional derivatives have been taken everything gets computed at the background, so that the distinction between
g_{\mu\nu}
and
\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}
disappears. For this reason we will basically write all the formalism below in terms of the metric
g_{\mu\nu}
with the understanding that it should be restricted to the on-shell unimodular background
\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}
satisfying (
5.10
).

Objects dual to those defined above, or raising and lowering condensed indices
{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}\;\mapsto\;(\mu\nu,x)
,
\alpha\;\mapsto\;(\alpha,x)
,
{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}\;\mapsto\;x
, can be attained by introducing a local configuration space metric
G_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}
and a similar metric in the space of gauge indices. In Einstein theory a natural choice is the DeWitt metric of the kinetic term of the action,
G_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\;\mapsto\;G^{\mu\nu,x\>\alpha\beta,y}=\tfrac{1}{2}g^{1/2}(g^{\mu(\alpha}g^{\beta)\nu}-\tfrac{1}{2}g^{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta})\delta(x,y)
. In the unimodular context, again due to projecting out the trace part of metric fluctuations, it is more useful to choose

\displaystyle G_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\,\;\mapsto\;\,G^{\mu\nu,x\>\alpha\beta,y}=\tfrac{1}{2}g^{1/2}g^{\mu(\alpha}g^{\beta)\nu}\delta(x,y).

(5.22)

As regards the sector of gauge and reducibility indices, the role of the relevant metrics can be played respectively by
\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}
and
\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
introduced in Sec.
II
. (Let us remind that by
{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}
we denote the inverse to
\varkappa^{\beta\alpha}
, and by
\kappa^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}
— the inverse to
\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
.)

The gauge-fixing choice (
5.17
) and (
5.18
) leads to operator (
2.42
) and dual operators (
4.37
)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\equiv\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}{\varkappa}_{\alpha\beta}\sigma^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,\;\mapsto\;\,\kappa_{xy}=-g^{1/2}\Box\delta(x,y),\;\;\;\;

(5.23)

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\!\sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\beta}\equiv\kappa^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\sigma^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}\,\;\mapsto\;\,\sigma^{x}_{\,\beta,y}=-\frac{1}{\Box}g^{-1/2}\nabla_{\beta}\delta(x,y),\qquad\;\;

(5.24)

which define the projector form (
2.45
)

\displaystyle\varPi_{\alpha\beta}\equiv\varkappa_{\alpha\beta}-{\varkappa}_{\alpha\gamma}\sigma^{\gamma}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\sigma^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\beta}\,\;\mapsto\;\,

\displaystyle\quad\varPi_{\alpha,x\>\beta,y}=\Big(g^{-1/2}g_{\alpha\beta}-\nabla_{\alpha}\frac{1}{\Box}g^{-1/2}\nabla_{\beta}\Big)\delta(x,y).\qquad\;\;

(5.25)

Partial derivatives acting on delta functions in (
5.24
) and (
V.1
) are covariant derivatives
\nabla_{\beta}
since they finally act on vector densities (forming a covariant divergence).

With the above objects the construction of the metric and ghost fields inverse propagators (
4.4
) and (
4.20
) is straightforward,

\displaystyle F_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\,\;\mapsto\;\,F^{\mu\nu\>\alpha\beta}(\nabla)\,\delta(x,y),

\displaystyle\qquad F^{\mu\nu\>\alpha\beta}(\nabla)=\tfrac{1}{2}g^{1/2}\Big(g^{\mu(\alpha}g^{\beta)\nu}\Box+2R^{\mu(\alpha\nu\beta)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad-2\nabla^{(\mu}\nabla^{\nu)}\frac{1}{\Box}\nabla^{(\alpha}\nabla^{\beta)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+g^{\mu\nu}\nabla^{(\alpha}\nabla^{\beta)}+g^{\alpha\beta}\nabla^{(\mu}\nabla^{\nu)}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad-g^{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}(\Box+\tfrac{1}{4}R)\Big),

(5.26)

\displaystyle F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}\,\;\mapsto\;\,F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}(\nabla)\,\delta(x,y),

\displaystyle\qquad F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}(\nabla)=g^{1/2}\Big((\Box+\tfrac{1}{4}R)\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}-\tfrac{1}{2}R\,\nabla^{\alpha}\frac{1}{\Box}\nabla_{\beta}\Big),

(5.27)

\displaystyle F^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=\delta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,\;\mapsto\;\,F^{x}_{\,y}=\delta(x,y).

(5.28)

In the derivation of these expressions we used background equations of motion (
5.10
), so these are essentially on-shell objects. In particular, applications beyond one-loop approximation would generate extra terms because of the necessity to distinguish background and quantum fields. The choice of the gauge-fixing matrix
\rho^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
in (
5.20
) allows one to avoid extra term
\nabla^{\alpha}\nabla_{\beta}
in the local part of the vector operator
F^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}(\nabla)
. Finally, simplification of the ghosts-for-ghosts operator
F^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\;{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
is due to correct normalization of reducibility generators (
4.27
) and a special choice (
5.19
) of
\omega^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}=Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}
.

Note that the operators acquired nonlocal parts containing inverse (scalar) d’Alembertians. They were generated due to nonlocal projectors in the gauge-breaking term and in the projected gauge generators. Moreover, even in the local part of the tensor operator covariant derivatives do not form overall d’Alembertian, and their indices get contracted with the indices of test functions on which the operator is acting. This situation is very different from the Einstein theory case for which DeWitt gauge conditions guarantee minimal nature of the operator of field disturbances which is an important property admitting direct use of the heat kernel method for the calculation of the effective action.

The local part of (
5.26
) can, however, be simplified. Its functional determinant enters the one-loop partition function via the combination (
4.12
),
\det F_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\,\det\varTheta^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
, or (
4.17
), where
\varTheta^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
is defined in terms of
F_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}
by Eq. (
4.9
). It is easy to check that this combination is invariant under the change of the operator
F_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}
of the form

\displaystyle\delta F_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\,=\,\delta\varOmega_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}({\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\,\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{,{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}})},

(5.29)

because

\displaystyle\delta\ln\big(\det F_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\,\det\varTheta^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\big)\,=\,\delta\varOmega_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\,\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{,{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\,F^{-1\,{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}

\displaystyle\qquad\;-\,\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}F^{-1\,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\delta\varOmega_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\,\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}_{,{\mathchoice{K}{K}{\scriptscriptstyle{K}}{K}}}F^{-1\,{\mathchoice{K}{K}{\scriptscriptstyle{K}}{K}}{\mathchoice{L}{L}{\scriptscriptstyle{L}}{L}}}\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{,{\mathchoice{L}{L}{\scriptscriptstyle{L}}{L}}}\varTheta^{-1}_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\,=\,0,\quad\;\;\;\;

(5.30)

since
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}_{,{\mathchoice{K}{K}{\scriptscriptstyle{K}}{K}}}F^{-1\,{\mathchoice{K}{K}{\scriptscriptstyle{K}}{K}}{\mathchoice{L}{L}{\scriptscriptstyle{L}}{L}}}\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{,{\mathchoice{L}{L}{\scriptscriptstyle{L}}{L}}}=\varTheta^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{c}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
. Therefore, we can omit in (
5.26
) the terms of the second line which are proportional to
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\sim g^{\mu\nu}
and
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{,{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\sim g^{\alpha\beta}
and replace this operator by

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}g^{1/2}\Big(\,g^{\mu(\alpha}g^{\beta)\nu}\Box+2R^{\mu(\alpha\nu\beta)}

(5.31)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-2\nabla^{(\mu}\nabla^{\nu)}\frac{1}{\Box}\nabla^{(\alpha}\nabla^{\beta)}\Big),

accordingly taking the operator
\tilde{\varTheta}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
induced by
\tilde{F}^{-1\,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}=\tilde{F}^{-1}_{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)\,\delta(x,y)
,

\displaystyle\!\!\tilde{\varTheta}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\tilde{F}^{-1\,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}_{,{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\,\;\mapsto\;\,\tilde{\varTheta}^{x\,y}=\tilde{\varTheta}(\nabla)\delta(x,y),\qquad

(5.32)

\displaystyle\!\!\tilde{\varTheta}(\nabla)\,=\,\tfrac{1}{4}g^{1/2}g^{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{-1}_{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)g^{\alpha\beta}g^{1/2}.

(5.33)

Still, all the vector (
5.27
), tensor (
5.31
) and scalar (
5.33
) operators remain nonlocal, and their remains a problem of reducing their determinants to some calculable form.

V.2
Reduction of functional determinants

Reduction of the above determinants can be done by the decomposition of the space of tensor and vector fields into irreducible transverse and traceless components. However, this decomposition in concrete applications can be useful only when the underlying metric background is homogeneous and the bases of irreducible scalar, transverse vector and tensor harmonics with their explicit spectra are known. If one wants to work on generic backgrounds and use such general methods as heat kernel method or Schwinger-DeWitt technique of curvature expansion
Schwinger-DeWitt
, then the above operators should be transformed to the form of differential or pseudod̃ifferential operators with simple principal symbols, preferably local and minimal ones that are constructed of covariant derivatives which form powers of covariant d’Alembertians. This is especially important for the operator (
5.33
) having essentially nonlocal structure with the Green’s function of another nonlocal operator.

In order to reduce the calculation of the determinant of
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)
to that of the minimal operator let us include it into the one-parameter family

\displaystyle\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(a|\nabla)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\tfrac{1}{2}g^{1/2}\Big(g^{\mu(\alpha}g^{\beta)\nu}\Box+2R^{\mu(\alpha\nu\beta)}

(5.34)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad-2a\nabla^{(\mu}\nabla^{\nu)}\frac{1}{\Box}\nabla^{(\alpha}\nabla^{\beta)}\Big),\quad

interpolating between
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(1|\nabla)=\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)
and the local minimal operator
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(0|\nabla)=\Delta^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)
,

\displaystyle\!\!\!\Delta_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\!=G_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{K}{K}{\scriptscriptstyle{K}}{K}}}\Delta^{\!{\mathchoice{K}{K}{\scriptscriptstyle{K}}{K}}}_{\>{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\!\;\mapsto\;\Delta^{\mu\nu\;\alpha\beta}(\nabla)\!=\!\tfrac{1}{2}g^{1/2}g^{\mu\gamma}g^{\nu{\,\delta}}\Delta_{\gamma{\,\delta}}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}(\nabla),

\displaystyle\qquad\Delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}(\nabla)\,=\,\Box\,\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}+2R_{\mu\;\;\nu}^{\,\,(\alpha\;\;\beta)}.

(5.35)

Differentiation of the family of their determinants gives

\displaystyle\hskip-14.22636pt\frac{d}{da}\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\ln\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(a|\nabla)

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt=-\!\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\Big(\,g^{1/2}\nabla^{(\alpha}\nabla^{\beta)}\frac{1}{\Box}\nabla^{(\mu}\nabla^{\nu)}\tilde{F}^{-1}_{\mu\nu\;\gamma\delta}(a|\nabla)\,\Big),\quad

(5.36)

where the double derivative of the inverse operator
\tilde{F}^{-1}_{\mu\nu\;\gamma\delta}(a|\nabla)
can be obtained by the following sequence of transformations. When applied to
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\;\alpha\beta}(a|\nabla)
this double derivative reads as a local expression

\displaystyle\!\!\!\!\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(a|\nabla)

\displaystyle\!\qquad=\,\tfrac{1}{4}g^{1/2}\big(\,2(1{-}2a)\Box+(1{-}a)R\,\big)\nabla^{(\alpha}\nabla^{\beta)},\qquad

(5.37)

where we used the fact that
\nabla_{\mu}R^{\mu\alpha\nu\beta}\!=0
on Einstein background.
Functionally contracting this relation with the inverse of
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\;\alpha\beta}(a|\nabla)
on the right and with the inverse of the scalar operator
g^{1/2}\big(2(1-2a)\Box+(1-a)R\big)
on the left, we obtain

\displaystyle\nabla^{\alpha}\nabla^{\beta}\tilde{F}^{-1}_{\alpha\beta\,\mu\nu}(a|\nabla)

\displaystyle\qquad=\,\frac{2}{\big(\Box+\frac{R}{2}\big)-2a\big(\Box+\frac{R}{4}\big)}g^{-1/2}\nabla_{(\mu}\nabla_{\nu)},\qquad

(5.38)

whence

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\nabla^{\alpha}\nabla^{\beta}\tilde{F}^{-1}_{\alpha\beta\,\mu\nu}(a|\nabla)\nabla^{\mu}\nabla^{\nu}

\displaystyle=\,2\frac{\Box\big(\Box+\frac{R}{4}\big)}{\big(\Box+\frac{R}{2}\big)-2a\big(\Box+\frac{R}{4}\big)}g^{-1/2},

(5.39)

where we took into account that on Einstein manifold
\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\nabla^{\mu}\nabla^{\nu}=\Box\big(\Box+\frac{R}{4}\big)
when this operator is acting on a scalar. Substituting this result in (
5.36
), using cyclic permutation under the trace and integrating over
a
from 0 to 1 we have

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\ln\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(1|\nabla)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}\ln\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(0|\nabla)

(5.40)

\displaystyle+\,{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}}^{\prime}\ln\frac{\Box}{\Box+\frac{R}{2}}.

Here
{\mathop{\mathrm{Tr}}}^{\prime}
implies taking the functional trace of the operator over the space of eigenmodes of the scalar operator
\Box

excluding
its constant zero mode. The explanation of this important fact follows from the observation that the action of the operator (
V.2
) on a covariantly constant mode obviously gives zero in view of the positive power of
\Box
in the numerator. Therefore, even the multiplication by
1/\Box
in (
V.2
) does not make the contribution of this mode in the functional trace nonzero; this is obviously consistent with the Penrose-Moore prescription for the inverse of
\Box
discussed above.

With the inclusion of the local measure factor (
5.40
) then becomes

\displaystyle\det\tilde{F}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\>{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}\,\;\mapsto\;\,

\displaystyle\qquad\det\tilde{F}_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}(\nabla)=\det\Delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}(\nabla)\,{\det}^{\prime}\frac{\Box}{\Box+\frac{R}{2}},\quad\;\;

(5.41)

where the prime in
{\det}^{\prime}
obviously implies the same rule — omission of the zero eigenvalue of
\Box
in the definition of the functional determinant, and this of course refers to both the numerator and denominator of the operator valued fraction under the sign of
{\det}^{\prime}
.

Similar steps for the vector operator (
5.27
) on the Einstein metric background result in

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\det\tilde{F}^{\alpha}_{\;\beta}(\nabla)=\det\big(\Box\,\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}+\tfrac{1}{4}R\,\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}\big)\,{\det}^{\prime}\frac{\Box}{\Box+\frac{R}{2}}.

(5.42)

The reduction of the determinant of the scalar operator
\tilde{\varTheta}(\nabla)
, which is defined by Eq. (
5.33
) and does not at all have a local part, can also be done via the transformations of the above type. First of all consider the contraction which localizes the operator
\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)
,

g_{\mu\nu}\tilde{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)=\tfrac{1}{2}g^{1/2}\Big(\,\big(\Box+\tfrac{1}{2}R\big)\,g^{\alpha\beta}-2\nabla^{\alpha}\nabla^{\beta}\,\Big).

(5.43)

Contracting this relation with
\tilde{F}^{-1}_{\alpha\beta\,\mu\nu}(\nabla)
and using Eq. (
5.38
) with the parameter
a=1
we get

g^{\alpha\beta}\tilde{F}^{-1}_{\alpha\beta\,\mu\nu}(\nabla)=\frac{2}{\Box+\frac{R}{2}}\,\Big(g_{\mu\nu}-\frac{2}{\Box}\nabla_{\mu}\nabla_{\nu}\Big)g^{-1/2},

(5.44)

so that the operator (
5.33
) and its determinant take the form

\tilde{\varTheta}(\nabla)=\frac{1}{\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}},\,\quad\,\det\tilde{\varTheta}(\nabla)=\frac{1}{\det\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big)}.

(5.45)

Assembling together in the tilde version of (
4.19
) the results (
V.2
), (
5.42
), (
5.45
), trivial contribution of
\det\rho=1
and noting that
\det\kappa_{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}={\det}^{\prime}\,\Box
we finally get

Z^{\rm 1-loop}_{\rm UMG}\!=\frac{\det\big(\Box\,\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}+\tfrac{R}{4}\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}\big)}{\!\big[\det(\Box\,\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}\!+2R_{\,\mu\,\;\nu}^{\,\,(\alpha\;\beta)}\!)\big]^{1/2}\!}\!\left[\,\frac{\det\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big)}{{\det}^{\prime}\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big)}\right]^{1/2}\!\!\!\!.

(5.46)

Note the origin of the nontrivial factor

\displaystyle\left[\,\frac{\det\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big)}{{\det}^{\prime}\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big)}\,\right]^{1/2}\!\!=\,(2\varLambda)^{1/2},

(5.47)

which looks purely numerical, but in fact it is a function of the dynamical global degree of freedom
\varLambda
belonging in UMG to the full configuration space of the theory.

Modulo this extra factor, the result (
5.46
) exactly coincides with the one-loop contribution of gravitons in Einstein theory with the action

\displaystyle S_{\varLambda}[\,g_{\mu\nu}]\,=\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}(R-2\varLambda)

(5.48)

and the on-shell value of the cosmological constant
\varLambda=R/4
,

\displaystyle\hat{Z}^{\rm 1-loop}_{\rm E}(\varLambda)=\frac{\det\big(\Box\,\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}+\tfrac{R}{4}\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}\big)}{\big[\det(\Box\,\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}+2R_{\mu\;\;\nu}^{\,\,(\alpha\;\;\beta)})\,\big]^{1/2}}\,\Bigg|_{\,R_{\mu\nu}=\varLambda g_{\mu\nu}}.

(5.49)

The on-shell inverse propagator of this model in the DeWitt gauge,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}(\varLambda\,|\,\nabla)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Big(\Box\,\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}+2R_{\mu\;\;\nu}^{\,\,(\alpha\;\;\beta)}+\delta_{\mu}^{(\alpha}R^{\beta)}_{\nu}+\delta_{\nu}^{(\alpha}R^{\beta)}_{\mu}

(5.50)

\displaystyle\quad-R_{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}-g_{\mu\nu}R^{\alpha\beta}+(2\varLambda-R)\,\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\quad+\tfrac{1}{2}R\,g_{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\Big)\Big|_{R_{\mu\nu}=\varLambda g_{\mu\nu}}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\Box\,\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}+2R_{\mu\;\;\nu}^{\,\,(\alpha\;\;\beta)},

equals the above minimal operator
\Delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}(\nabla)
and the Faddeev-Popov ghost operator
Q^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}=\Box\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}+R^{\alpha}_{\beta}
also coincides with the vector operator
(\Box+\tfrac{1}{4}R)\delta^{\alpha}_{\,\beta}
. So if we consider Einstein gravity with the cosmological constant as a parental theory of unimodular gravity then the relation (
5.46
) can be interpreted as Eq. (
4.53
) relating the generating functionals of the restricted theory and the parental one with
\hat{Z}^{\rm 1-loop}\,\mapsto\,\hat{Z}^{\rm 1-loop}_{\rm E}(\varLambda)
, provided we can prove equality of factors (
5.47
) and
(\det\varTheta^{AB})^{-1/2}
. This proof is straightforward.

The only invariant
\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}
that can be built out of the restriction function (
5.4
)-(
5.5
) by integrating it with the constant zero mode of the gauge-restriction operator is the following global quantity which reads along with its
\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}
as

\displaystyle\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}\equiv\int d^{4}x\;\theta(x)=\int d^{4}x\;(g^{1/2}(x)-1),\quad\;\;

(5.51)

\displaystyle\theta^{\mathchoice{A}{A}{\scriptscriptstyle{A}}{A}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}

\displaystyle\;\mapsto\;

\displaystyle\bar{\theta}^{\mu\nu}(y)=\tfrac{1}{2}g^{1/2}g^{\mu\nu}(y).

(5.52)

In order to find its
\varTheta^{AB}
,

\bar{\varTheta}^{AB}\,\;\mapsto\;\,\bar{\varTheta}\equiv\int d^{4}x\,d^{4}y\,\bar{\theta}^{\mu\nu}(x)\hat{F}^{-1}_{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)\delta(x,y)\bar{\theta}^{\alpha\beta}(y),

(5.53)

we need the gauge field inverse propagator of the parental theory — Einstein gravity with a cosmological constant, which reads on shell as

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}{\mathchoice{J}{J}{\scriptscriptstyle{J}}{J}}}

\displaystyle\mapsto

\displaystyle\hat{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)\,\big|_{\,R_{\mu\nu}=\varLambda g_{\mu\nu}}

(5.54)

\displaystyle\;=\tfrac{1}{4}\,g^{1/2}\big(2g^{\mu(\alpha}g^{\beta)\nu}-g^{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\big)\Box

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;+g^{1/2}\big(R^{\mu(\alpha\nu\beta)}-\tfrac{1}{8}\,R\,g^{\mu\nu}g^{\alpha\beta}\big).

This operator satisfies an obvious relation

g_{\mu\nu}\hat{F}^{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)\,=\,-\tfrac{1}{2}\,g^{1/2}\big(\Box+\tfrac{1}{2}R\big)g^{\alpha\beta},

(5.55)

which allows one to find the following contraction of the Green’s function kernel with metric tensors

g^{\mu\nu}\hat{F}^{-1}_{\mu\nu\,\alpha\beta}(\nabla)\delta(x,y)g^{\alpha\beta}(y)\,=\frac{-8}{\Box+\tfrac{1}{2}R}\delta(x,y)g^{-1/2}(y),

(5.56)

whence the needed factor (
5.53
) equals

\bar{\varTheta}=-2\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}(x)\frac{1}{\Box+\tfrac{1}{2}R}{\cdot}1\,=-\frac{1}{\varLambda}\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}(x),

(5.57)

where the last equality follows from the uniformity of
R=4\varLambda
in spacetime. Of course on shell,
g_{\mu\nu}=\textsl{g}_{\mu\nu}
, we have
g^{1/2}=1
, so the square root of the metric determinant is retained entirely for the sake of manifest covariance, and in this way it represents invariant volume of spacetime. Thus,

(\det\varTheta^{AB})^{-1/2}\,\;\mapsto\;\,\bar{\varTheta}^{-1/2}=\frac{\varLambda^{1/2}}{\big(\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}(x)\big)^{1/2}},

(5.58)

which up to a constant factor coincides with (
5.47
).
15
15

15

When calculating determinants we omit irrelevant overall numerical factors. Here in the determinant of a zero-dimensional matrix we neglect
-1
factor.

For completeness we present here the same answer rewritten in the basis of irreducible subspaces of tensor and vector fields, which are defined via disentangling from the full tensor field its tranverse-traceless
h^{\rm TT}=h_{\mu\nu}^{\rm TT}
, transversal vector
h_{\rm T}=h_{\mu}^{\rm T}
and two scalar parts
York:1974psa
,

\displaystyle h_{{\mu}{\nu}}=h^{\rm TT}_{{\mu}{\nu}}+2\nabla_{({\mu}}h^{\rm T}_{{\nu})}+\big(\nabla_{{\mu}}\nabla_{{\nu}}-\tfrac{1}{4}g_{{\mu}{\nu}}\Box\big)s+\tfrac{1}{4}g_{\mu\nu}h,

\displaystyle\nabla^{\mu}h^{\rm TT}_{{\mu}{\nu}}=g^{\mu\nu}h^{\rm TT}_{{\mu}{\nu}}=0,\,\quad\,\,\nabla^{\mu}h^{\rm T}_{\mu}=0.

(5.59)

Similarly for a vector such a decomposition reads

\displaystyle v_{\mu}=v_{\mu}^{\rm T}+\nabla_{\mu}v,\,\quad\,\,\nabla^{\mu}v_{\mu}^{\rm T}=0.

(5.60)

Jacobians of transition
h_{\mu\nu}\to(h^{\rm TT},h^{\rm T},s,h)
,
v_{\mu}\to(v_{\mu}^{\rm T},v)
in the functional integration over
h_{\mu\nu}
and
v_{\mu}
equal

\displaystyle\frac{D(h_{\mu\nu})}{D(h^{\rm TT},h^{\rm T},s,h)}

\displaystyle=\Big({\det}^{\prime}\Box\>{\det}^{\prime}\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{3}\big)\det\!_{\rm T}\big(\Box\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}+\tfrac{R}{4}\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}\big)\Big)^{1/2}\!\!\!\!,\qquad

(5.61)

\displaystyle\frac{D(v_{\mu})}{D(v^{\rm T},v)}\,=\,\big({\det}^{\prime}\,\Box\,\big)^{1/2}.

(5.62)

Here
{\det}^{\prime}
is a functional determinant of a scalar operator with omitted zero mode of the
\Box
-operator (note that this is the omission of the (constant) zero mode of
\Box
, but
not
the zero mode of the operator whose determinant is being taken).
16
16

16

The omission of these spacetime constant zero modes of
\Box
takes place because they do not contribute to the left-hand sides of Eqs. (
V.2
)–(
5.60
).

Similarly
\det\!_{\rm T}
denotes the determinant of the vector operator taken on the space of transverse vector functions. Quadratic forms with tensor field
\Delta^{\mu\nu\;\alpha\beta}(\nabla)
and vector field
(\Box+R/4)g_{\mu\nu}
kernels correspondingly read

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}h_{\mu\nu}\Delta^{\mu\nu\;\alpha\beta}(\nabla)h_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\;\;=\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}\Big(\,h^{\rm TT}_{\mu\nu}\Delta^{\mu\nu\;\alpha\beta}(\nabla)h^{\rm TT}_{\alpha\beta}-h_{\mu}^{\rm T}\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{4}\big)^{2}h^{\mu}_{\rm T}

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\;\;\;\;+\frac{3}{4}s\Box(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2})(\Box+\tfrac{R}{3})s+\tfrac{1}{4}h(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2})h\,\Big),

(5.63)

\displaystyle\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}v_{\mu}\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{4}\big)v^{\mu}

\displaystyle\;\;=\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}\Big(\,v_{\mu}^{\rm T}\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{4}\big)v^{\mu}_{\rm T}-v\Box\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big)v\,\Big).\quad

(5.64)

Taking the Gaussian integrals with these quadratic forms and using the above Jacobians one can find the representation for the determinants of Eq. (
5.46
) in terms of their irreducible counterpart — transverse-traceless tensor
\det\!_{\rm TT}
and transverse vector
\det\!_{\rm T}
ones,

\displaystyle\det\big(\Box\,\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}+2R_{\mu\;\;\nu}^{\,\,(\alpha\;\;\beta)}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\;\;=\det\!_{\rm TT}\big(\Box\,\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}+2R_{\mu\;\;\nu}^{\,\,(\alpha\;\;\beta)}\big)\det\!_{\rm T}\big(\Box\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}+\tfrac{R}{4}\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;\;\times\det\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big)\,{\det}^{\prime}\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big),

(5.65)

\displaystyle\det\big(\Box\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}\!+\tfrac{R}{4}\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}\!\big)

\displaystyle\qquad\;\;=\det\!_{\rm T}\big(\Box\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}+\tfrac{R}{4}\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}\big)\,{\det}^{\prime}\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big),

(5.66)

whence the partition function in Einstein theory with the cosmological constant (
5.49
) reads

\displaystyle\hat{Z}^{\rm 1-loop}_{\rm E}=\left[\,\frac{\det\!_{\rm T}\big(\Box\,\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}+\tfrac{R}{4}\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}\big)}{\det\!_{\rm TT}\big(\Box\,\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}+2R_{\mu\;\;\nu}^{\,\,(\alpha\;\;\beta)}\big)}\,\right]^{1/2}

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\;\;\times\left[\,\frac{{\det}^{\prime}\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big)}{{\det}\,\big(\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}\big)}\,\right]^{1/2}.

(5.67)

In terms of these determinants on irreducible subspaces of transverse-traceless modes it differs by extra factor from the usually claimed form
Tseytlin
. This factor originates on account of a constant zero mode of a scalar d’Alembertian. It should be emphasized that on homogeneous de Sitter or anti-de Sitter background other vector and tensor operators also have zero modes associated with Killing symmetries of these backgrounds (see e.g.
Tseytlin
). Here we disregard them because we consider generic inhomogeneous Einstein metric spacetimes for all of which this zero mode of
\Box
always exists.

Thus, in terms of determinants on constrained (irreducible) fields the one-loop result for Einstein theory with the cosmological constant differs from a conventional expression by the contribution of one constant eigenmode of the operator
\Box+\tfrac{R}{2}
. Curiously, for unimodular gravity this contribution in the same representation completely cancels by additional factor in (
5.46
), and we have

\displaystyle\hskip-22.76228ptZ^{\rm 1-loop}_{\rm UMG}=\left[\,\frac{\det\!_{\rm T}\big(\Box\,\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}+\tfrac{R}{4}\delta^{\mu}_{\,\nu}\big)}{\det\!_{\rm TT}\big(\Box\,\delta_{\mu\nu}^{\;\;\alpha\beta}+2R_{\mu\;\;\nu}^{\,\,(\alpha\;\;\beta)}\big)}\,\right]^{1/2}\!\!.

(5.68)

This result coincides with the one claimed in
deLeonArdon:2017qzg
;
Percacci:2017fsy
. It manifestly exhibits the counting of local physical degrees of freedom — 5 traceless-tensor modes minus 3 transverse vector modes.

VI
Conclusion

To summarize our results, we worked out a full set of gauge-fixing elements in generic gauge theory of the first-stage reducibility and constructed a workable algorithm for its one-loop effective action. We also derived the set of tree-level Ward identities for gauge field, ghost and ghosts-for-ghosts propagators, which allow one to prove on-shell gauge independence of the effective action from the choice of auxiliary elements of gauge-fixing procedure. We showed that Lagrangian quantization of a restricted theory originating from its parental gauge theory can be performed within the BV formalism for models with linearly-dependent gauge generators of the first-stage reducibility. It turns out that new physics contained in the restricted theory as compared to its parental theory model is associated with the rank deficiency of a special gauge-restriction operator reflecting the gauge transformation properties of the restriction constraints functions. The choice of first-stage reducibility generators, or zero vectors of the projected gauge generators induced from the parental theory, has a certain freedom limited only by a special rank restriction condition, but the on-shell independence of physical results from this choice is provided by a special normalization of these vectors.

These general results are applied to the quantization of unimodular gravity theory. Its one-loop effective action, initially obtained in terms of complicated nonlocal pseudodifferential operators, is transformed to functional determinants of minimal second-order differential operators calculable on generic backgrounds by Schwinger-DeWitt technique of local curvature expansion. This also confirms the known representation of one-loop contribution in unimodular gravity theory in terms of functional determinants on irreducible transverse and transverse traceless subspaces of tensor, vector, and scalar modes. The one-loop order in unimodular gravity turns out to be equivalent to that of Einstein gravity theory with a cosmological term only up to a special contribution of the global degree of freedom associated with the variable value of the cosmological constant.

From the viewpoint of local phenomenology, the new physics in unimodular gravity turns out to be of a somewhat borderline nature. Classically it is manifested in the fact that the cosmological constant in UMG becomes a part of initial conditions rather than a fundamental constant in the Lagrangian of the Einstein theory. At the one-loop level the contribution of this extra global and spacetime constant degree of freedom is very peculiar, and the way it shows up depends on the representation of the theory. In fact we have two somewhat complementary representations for both theories: in terms of functional determinants of differential operators on full field spaces or on spaces constrained by irreducible representations. The one-loop Einstein gravity in the full space representation (
5.49
) does not reveal the contribution of this mode, whereas the constrained determinants representation (
V.2
) makes it manifest. With two representations for unimodular gravity (
5.46
) and (
5.68
) this situation is reversed.

The manifestation of this contribution is the power-like dependence on (
5.57
) in the partition function
\sim\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}/\varLambda
which becomes in the effective action a logarithmic essentially nonlocal contribution. Off shell, that is in transition to gradient expansion for nonconstant curvature scalar,
R\to R(x)
, it may go over into the structures like
\ln\int d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}(x)\big(1/R(x)+O(\nabla R)\big)
. These structures might be important in Euclidean quantum gravity responsible for tunneling phenomena and gravitational thermodynamics. In fact, thermodynamics reveals the duality relation between Einstein theory and unimodular gravity as the analogy of the Laplace transform relating the statistical ensemble with fixed volume vs the fixed pressure ensemble. Qualitatively, this can be shown as follows.

By identifying in (
5.11
) the coordinate 4-volume
\int d^{4}x=V
as the fixed argument of the generating functional (
4.52
) we have the expression for this functional in UMG theory (in Euclidean picture this is a partition function at fixed volume
V
)

\displaystyle Z_{\rm UMG}(V)

\displaystyle\;=\;

\displaystyle\int Dg_{\mu\nu}\hat{M}\,e^{-S_{E}[\,g_{\mu\nu}]}

(6.1)

\displaystyle\qquad\times\,\delta\big({\textstyle\int}d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}-V\big),

where the measure
\hat{M}
incorporates, just like in (
4.52
), the full local gauge fixing of all four dimensional diffeomorphisms. Then, it is obvious that the Laplace transform with respect to the volume variable converts the UMG partition function
Z_{\rm UMG}(V)
to that of the Einstein theory
Z_{\rm E}(\varLambda)
with a fixed value of
\varLambda
— the cosmological constant dual to
V
,

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{\infty}dV\,e^{-\frac{\varLambda V}{8\pi G}}Z_{\rm UMG}(V)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;=\int Dg_{\mu\nu}\,\hat{M}\,\exp\big(-S_{E}[\,g_{\mu\nu}]-\tfrac{\varLambda}{8\pi G}{\textstyle\int}d^{4}x\,g^{1/2}\big)

\displaystyle\;\;\;\;\equiv\,Z_{\rm E}(\varLambda),

(6.2)

(here we reinsert the gravitational constant factor
1/16\pi G
and note the opposite sign of the Euclidean version of the action (
5.48
)). Of course, this derivation should be regulated by specifying the boundary conditions which fully determine a finite value of
V
(or its infinite limit) and a finite value of the action, achieved by a subtraction of proper surface terms. This can be done along the lines of
Fiol:2008vk
, but in the present form it already conveys the essence of duality between Einstein theory and UMG gravity.

It should be emphasized that throughout our derivations we used Moore-Penrose concept of inverting operators with zero modes, which by and large corresponds to the omission of the zero-mode subspace. This makes all derivations, as discussed in Appendix
A
, consistent, but apparently leaves room for nontrivial effects of the above extra contributions based on a careful treatment of boundary conditions. Despite the fact that the functional determinants of the scalar d’Alembertian in Eqs. (
V.2
)–(
V.2
), which are vulnerable to zero-mode treatment, completely cancel out, there still may be a subtlety in their treatment and this might amount to the extension of the BV method beyond first-stage reducibility. Note that
m_{2}
-dimensional zero-mode subspace of gauge-restriction operator (
3.11
) is exactly the playground for second-stage reducibility in the general formalism. The smallness of the phase-space sector of this mode in UMG,
m_{2}=1
, does not make it less important and might be at the core of cosmological constant problem. All this, however, goes beyond the scope of this paper and remains a subject of further research to be reported elsewhere.

Another direction of further research might be the generalized unimodular gravity (GUMG) of
Barvinsky:2017pmm
;
Barvinsky:2019agh
, which is interesting in view of its dark energy and inflation theory implications. This model is more complicated than UMG, it has more complicated canonical formalism encumbered by the presence of second-class Dirac constraints and it strongly breaks diffeomorphism and Lorentz symmetry because of replacement of the UMG restriction condition
\det g_{\mu\nu}=-1
by the Lorentz noninvariant relation between the lapse function and spatial metric. New physics in this model is associated with the origin of the dark perfect fluid which might serve as a source of dark energy or play the role of inflaton, i.e. scalar graviton degree of freedom
Barvinsky:2019qzx
. Covariant quantization of this model along the lines of the BV method applied to parental Einstein gravity is also a good nontrivial playground for our technique.

Appendix A
Moore-Penrose inverse and variation of projectors

Gauge theory restriction implies introduction of the transversal projector (
3.22
) which is defined by its left kernel
Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}
— the gauge-restriction operator
Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}=\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}_{\alpha}
.
17
17

17

Remember that we consider the restriction conditions
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}=0
irreducible and independent of equations of motion of the parental theory
\hat{S}_{,{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}=0
.
Peculiar feature of this operator is its
rank deficiency
. While the range of index
{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}
labelling the restriction condition functions
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}(\varphi)
is
m_{1}
, the rank of
Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\beta}
is
m_{1}-m_{2}
. As discussed in Sec.
III
it is physically important, for if
Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\beta}
were a full-rank matrix (
m_{2}=0
) then this would be just a partial gauge fixing and the parental and restricted theories would be physically equivalent. New physics comes when
m_{2}>0
, which means that
m_{2}
out of
m_{1}
restriction conditions
\theta^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}(\varphi)=0
annihilate several gauge-invariant functions — physical observables.

Right kernel
k^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
of the projector (
3.22
) is a parameter of the particular projector family, which must satisfy the rank condition
\rank{(Qk)^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}}=\rank{Q^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}}=\rank{k^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}}=m_{1}-m_{2}
(
3.23
).
Thus, critically important feature of the projector
T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}(Q,k)
(
3.22
),

T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}\,=\,\delta^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}-k^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}{{(Qk)^{-1}}\vphantom{|}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}Q^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta},

is that it includes the
inverse
of the
degenerate
operator
(Qk)^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
. This inverse can be uniquely defined as Moore-Penrose inverse
MoorePenrose
, and a brief reminder on this construction for the generalized inverse of generic matrices is in order here.

All objects below are of matrix nature so for the sake of readability we omit indices in most of equations implying ordinary matrix multiplication. When introducing matrices we explicitly correlate them with their counterparts in index notation using symbol
\leftrightarrow
.

For generic
m_{1}\times m_{1}
matrix
M\,\leftrightarrow\,M^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
which is rank deficient with
\rank M=m_{1}-m_{2}
the following properties unambiguously define Moore-Penrose inverse
{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\,\leftrightarrow\,{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}

MoorePenrose

\displaystyle\begin{array}[]{cl}1)&M{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}M=M\\
2)&{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}M{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}={M^{-1\!}}\\
3)&P_{1}\equiv M{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}=P_{1}^{*}\\
4)&P_{2}\equiv{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}M=P_{2}^{*}\\
\end{array}

where
P_{1}\,\leftrightarrow\,{P_{1}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
and
P_{2}\,\leftrightarrow\,{P_{2}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
are Hermitian projectors with idempotent properties
P_{1}^{2}=P_{1}
,
P_{2}^{2}=P_{2}
, and the star denotes Hermitian conjugation of matrices.
18
18

18

For real matrices the Hermitian conjugation is just a transposition, and Hermitian projectors become orthonormal projectors.
These properties imply the equality of ranks,
\rank{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}=\rank M=\rank P_{1}=\rank P_{2}
.

Introduce for convenience the complementary projectors
L_{1}=I-P_{1}
,
L_{2}=I-P_{2}
where
I
is the identity
m_{1}\times m_{1}
matrix. The complete set of projector properties, which could serve as their definitions, is

\displaystyle P_{1}M=MP_{2}=M,\,\quad\,{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}P_{1}=P_{2}{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}={M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}.

(A6)

Similarly the action of complimentary projectors respectively from the left and from the right on
M
and
{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}
annihilate them,

L_{1}M=ML_{2}=0,\,\quad\,{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}L_{1}=L_{2}{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}=0.

(A7)

Therefore, the rows of
L_{1}
(reducibly) span left kernel of
M
and its columns span right kernel of
{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}
, while
L_{2}
(reducibly) spans the right kernel of
M
and the left kernel of
{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}
.

With this definition of the inverse matrix for
M^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}=(Qk)^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\equiv Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}k^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,\leftrightarrow\,M=(Qk)
the restricted theory
m_{0}\times m_{0}
projector
T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}\,\leftrightarrow\,T
(
3.22
) satisfies all needed properties. In particular, its left kernel is spanned by the gauge-restriction operator
Q\,\leftrightarrow\,Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}
because
QT=Q-(Qk)(Qk)^{-1}Q=L_{1}Q=0
. The latter equality is true since matrices
Q
and
M=(Qk)
have the same ranks and the same coranks with respect to the left index with range
m_{1}
and thus matrices
Q
and
M
share the same left kernel so that
L_{1}Q=0
in view of (
A7
). The right kernel of
T
is spanned by
k\,\leftrightarrow\,k^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
provided the rank restriction condition (
3.23
) is satisfied. This follows from the relation
Tk=k-k(Qk)^{-1}(Qk)=kL_{2}=0
where
k
is annihilated by
L_{2}
from the right for the analogous reason as for the left kernel.

Such a definition is fully consistent until one has to vary inverse matrices. When the variation of
M
changes its rank the inverse matrix and projectors become discontinuous and fail to be differentiable. As was shown in
StewartIzumino
the rank preserving matrix variations
\delta M
which guarantee continuity of the Moore-Penrose inverse matrix and its projectors should satisfy the property

L_{1}\delta ML_{2}=0.

(A8)

The variation of equation 2) in (
A
) with respect to such
\delta M
gives

\displaystyle\!\!\!\delta\big({M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\big)=-{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\delta M{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\!\hskip-0.5pt+\delta\big({M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\big)L_{1}\!\hskip-0.5pt+L_{2}\delta\big({M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\big)\hskip-0.5pt.\;\;\;\;

(A9)

The last two
anomalous
terms here are responsible for the deviation from the usual variational equation for the inverse of nondegenerate matrices. The first standard term is in fact transverse because in view of (
A6
)
{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\delta M{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}=P_{2}{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\delta M{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}P_{1}
.

It is important that the anomaly terms in (
A9
) cancel when the variation of the inverse matrix is performed inside the contraction from the left and from the right respectively with structures annihilated by projectors
L_{2}
and
L_{1}
, so that
A=AP_{2}
and
B=P_{1}B
,

\displaystyle A\,\delta\big({M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\big)B\,

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-AP_{2}{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\delta M{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}P_{1}B

(A10)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle-A{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}\delta M{M^{-1\!}}\vphantom{|}B.

In concrete applications generically one can not restrict the variation of the rank-deficient matrix
M
to preserve its rank. However, when the matrix
M
has a special internal structure it may be a part of the solution circumventing this difficulty. This is exactly the case of
m_{1}\times m_{1}
rank-deficient operator
(Qk)^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\equiv Q^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,\alpha}k^{\alpha}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
. The rank restriction conditions (
3.23
),
\rank{(Qk)}=\rank{Q}=\rank{k}
, provide the rank preservation (
A8
) under arbitrary variations of
k
and
Q
,

{L_{1}}\delta(Qk){L_{2}}={L_{1}}\delta Q\,k{L_{2}}+{L_{1}}Q\,\delta k\,{L_{2}}=0,

(A11)

where
{L_{1}}
,
{L_{2}}
are the projectors onto left and right kernels of the operator
(Qk)
.

Since an arbitrary variation of the matrix
(Qk)^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}
satisfies the rank preserving condition
A8
one can use Eq. (
A9
) for the variation of its inverse. Moreover in the
projector
T
(
3.22
) the variation of
{(Qk)^{-1}}\vphantom{|}
stands inside the structure
k\,\delta{{(Qk)^{-1}}\vphantom{|}}Q\,\leftrightarrow\,k^{\beta}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}}\delta{{(Qk)^{-1}}\vphantom{|}}^{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{a}}}_{\,{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}\,Q^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}

which, due to
{L_{1}}Q=0
and

k\,{L_{2}}=0
, guarantees the cancelation of the anomalous terms in (
A9
) and implies (
A10
)

k\,\delta{{(Qk)^{-1}}\vphantom{|}}Q^{{{\color[rgb]{0.28,0.02,0.03}{b}}}}_{\,\beta}\,=\,-k{{(Qk)^{-1}}\vphantom{|}}\delta{(Qk)}{{(Qk)^{-1}}\vphantom{|}}Q.

(A12)

This variational equation underlies the variation formula for the projector
T
(
3.22
), which finally reads

\displaystyle\delta T\,=\,-T\,\delta k\,{{(Qk)^{-1}}\vphantom{|}}Q-k\,{(Qk)^{-1}}\vphantom{|}\delta Q\,T.

(A13)

It should be emphasized again that this relation crucially relies on rank restriction conditions (
3.23
).

Appendix B
Gauge algebra of the restricted theory from a parental theory

The algebra (
3.34
) follows directly by substituting the projected generators
\mathcal{R}^{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}_{\beta}=\hat{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathchoice{I}{I}{\scriptscriptstyle{I}}{I}}}_{\alpha}T^{\alpha}_{\>\beta}
(
3.20
) into their Lie bracket,
